The clearance of their goods through the customhouse is delayed by 'some difference betweene this Governour and his sonne in lawe, Monseir Chaun [<em>see</em> p. 190], with the Sheike' [<em>shaikh</em>]. 'Meirza Backer' [Mīrzā Bāqir] came hither yesterday to resume his place at the custom-house. They went out to meet him and found him 'sitting att the tombes'. He is to take up his post to-morrow, when he promises to clear their goods. (<em>Copy. 1 p. Received March 10.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Andrew Warden's Account of the Homeward Voyage of the</span> <em>William</em> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xlv. p. 63).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 2. 'Cometh a Porteguall aborde and tolde us veri strange neues conser[n]eing thear shipeing and thear forse thay have alongeste the coste, and what we may doe at Bumebaye; but mestrusten of him that he was seinte for some spye, and our shipeing to be desparesed to goe abrode and leave the <em>Exc[h]ange</em> and we in <em>Weillem</em> in the Holle and to take in our ladein, ded make a doute of him (as reseing ded requeir); exeamein[ing] of him, found him in towe or three severall talles, kepeing him in the ioreines [irons], and the nexte day holead his hede and ded burne his fingeres and his towes with maches, and hee ded confese a great deille of velenie and howe hee was seinte for a spye to see our foreses.' He was placed on board the <em>Mary</em> as a prisoner, to be released when she put to sea. <em>March</em> 11. Captain Hall's squadron went out of the Hole. <em>March</em> 21. The <em>Exchange</em> and two Dutch ships sailed for the Red Sea. <em>April</em> 6. The <em>William</em> fell down to the foot of the bar. <em>April</em> 15. Sailed. <em>June</em> 3. Saw the island of Rodriguez. <em>June</em> 7. Anchored at Mauritius. <em>June</em> 18. Sailed again. <em>June</em> 21. Passed an island. <em>August</em> 13. Consultation held by Captain Christopher Brown, Captain Kerridge, and the other merchants and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-304" data-page="304" data-folio="250" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n303_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 250">[p. 250]</button> officers; the ship being leaky and in bad condition, it was decided to bear up [for Madagascar]. <em>August</em> 17. Resolved to make for the Cape again. <em>August</em> 24. Land seen. <em>September</em> 1. Got into Table Bay. Caught some seals on Penguin [Robben] Island, and made a hogshead of lamp oil. Took in more ballast and a supply of water.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-070" href="#sec-08-fn-070" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-070" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>September</em> 19. Set sail. <em>October</em> 6. Anchored off Chapel Valley, St. Helena. <em>October</em> 13. Sailed again, having on board a number of live pigs and hogs caught on the island. <em>November</em> 29. Spoke a Dutch man-of-war. <em>November</em> 30. Saw Flores. <em>December</em> 11. Got into Falmouth. <em>December</em> 26. Sailed again. <em>December</em> 28. Anchored in the Downs. <em>December</em> 30. Set sail. <em>December</em> 31. Anchored in the Gore. 1629, <em>February</em> 14. Moored at Erith. (42½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Kerridge, Richard Wylde, and John Skibbow at Surat to the President and Council at Bantam, March</span> 8, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Java</em>, vol. iii. part ii. p. 461).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last by the <em>Blessing</em> [<em>see</em> p. 222]. Since then nothing of importance has happened. The English fleet reached Persia three days before the Dutch and also got back to Swally three days ahead, bringing 780 bales of silk, ‘amounting to above 60,000<em>l.</em> starling.’ Hope therefore to dispatch the <em>William</em>, fully laden, about the middle of April. ‘The <em>Mary</em>, <em>Hopwell</em>, and <em>Refuge</em>, whoe in company of the <em>Blessing</em> set sayle from hence alongest the Indian coast, returned without other effect then the discovery of som ports, as well of the Decanee and Portugalls, and assurance of those of Dabull, confederated with the Portugalls, will admitt of no trade without compositione. Ouer many shipps here wanting imployment, wee have sent part of the <em>Maryes</em> fleete in discovery and againe to range the coast, and after to winter in the islands of Saint Laurence and Commoro, in hope theyr indevores and suckses will prove better then theirs sent theither last yere.’ The <em>Exchange</em> is intended for the Red Sea (‘with the remaines were provided for you’), and will sail in six or eight days. Have given directions to those on board the <em>Mary</em> to advise Bantam of their proceedings prior to the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-305" data-page="305" data-folio="251" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n304_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 251">[p. 251]</button> departure of the <em>Refuge</em>. ‘The motives inducinge our sendinge this shipp unto [you] is occasioned through the hope of our fortefynge in som convenient harboure one this coast ; for which attempt when we came to consider of matterial we fownd our storehouse wholy destitute, even of those things were directly consigned hether by the <em>Palsgrave</em> and <em>Dolphine</em>, as spades, pickaxes, feild carrages, and all ells fittinge ; which at the shipps arrivall being demanded of the commanders, they avered to have receved non, except feild carage[s], which it semes they also left with you at Batavia, for here they ar not to be found. Captain Brown doth affirme that the said materiall[s] were sent on the <em>Expedicone</em>; which is not unlikly, seing the Company doe inorder you in there last letter to send hether all provisiones of that kind ; which in regard wee required not in the <em>Blessinge</em>, nor semed then to have any purpose of theire use, we have sent this vessell express to give you intelegence of our instant desier, vitz. for all municione and matterialls requesitt to such a desine, as great ordnance, powder, iron shott, ladells, spi[ ]nges, cartriges, feild carrages, musketts, bandelers [for ?] muskett shott, rests for musketts, pouches, wormes, scowrers, swords, pistoles, girdells, hangers, belts, scabords, chapes for swords, saws, filles, chesells, broad axes, adses, spicks and nailes of all sorts, planck, sheathinge boards, laths, and whatsoever else the Company sent forth for supply of Lagundy or the rendevoes, and dischargde of shipinge hath furnished your storehouse withall.’ Should this letter arrive before the <em>Blessing</em> departs, the stores may be laden in her, and the <em>Refuge</em> retained ; otherwise, the latter or some better ship must be dispatched with them, reaching Surat by September 20 or 24. No news received from Masulipatam, ‘although we have signified the Companis purpose concerning desolucion of that factory.’ The fine blue calicoes promised in their last have not yet arrived from Broach, but will be sent in the Dutch ships. Would be glad of twenty or thirty butts of arrack. (<em>Copy.</em> 3 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">An Account by Richard Predys</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-071" href="#sec-08-fn-071" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-071" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">of the Cruise of Captain Hall’s Fleet to the Comoros</span> (<em>O. C.</em> 1269).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 11. The <em>Mary</em> left Swally Hole. <em>March</em> 12. The fleet set sail. <em>March</em> 15. Passing Chaul, they anchored off <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-306" data-page="306" data-folio="252" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n305_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 252">[p. 252]</button> ‘Danda.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-072" href="#sec-08-fn-072" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-072" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> The captain of the castle, ‘Seidiambar,’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-073" href="#sec-08-fn-073" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-073" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> sent them a complimentary letter, with a present of cattle, fruit, &amp;c. At night another boat brought a present from ‘Abiscan’ [<em>see</em> p. 243], who was still besieging the castle on behalf of the King of ‘Decannee’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-074" href="#sec-08-fn-074" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-074" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> It was resolved to send Sīdī Ambar a present next day, ‘and to see if we could upon composition draw him to surrender up the castle unto us.’ <em>March 16.</em> Signor Jeronimo, Captain Altham, and Mr. Latch went ashore accordingly, with a chest of rosewater and four swordblades, and were courteously received by Sīdī Ambar. ‘Our people had some conference with him, and profferred him great rewards if he would deliver it up; but by noe meanes he would not be seduced to deliver up the castle; but promised, if we would retorne thether the next yeare, he would procure us a ferman from the King of Decan his master for our licence to trade in those partes, and alsoe would provide us a place where we might fortifie ourselves and keepe us out of danger from the Portugalls or any other enymie. But I conceave what he spoke was rather to give us content then otherwise, fearing we should have joyned against him with Abiscan; for, he keepeing the castle against the King, it is very unlike he wilbe able to doe for us anything he promised. Had we had tyme and more men to have landed, we might have peradventure taken it, for the seamen were once partely resolved to attempt the takeing of it; but after they had heard the relation of Captain Altham and one Latch, two land soldiers, they gave over their designe. . . . The castle of Danda is scituated in the sea upon a litle hommock, distant from the shoare a little more then a muskett shot; by nature very strong; wherein are at least 400 men, six great peeces of ordinance, and some 16 or 18 falconet and ravenet; envyroned with a wall, of 18 or 20 foote towards the land and some 14 foote towards the sea, round about, with battlements and halfe moones; and upon the top and middle of it a great house, and by it a block house, from whence we sawe <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-307" data-page="307" data-folio="253" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n306_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 253">[p. 253]</button> three peeces of ordinance shott over to the S. side unto two Mallabarr friggitts which were coming into the baye.’ Meanwhile the boats sounded all round the castle and between it and the main; but were not allowed to do the same further in, although on the previous occasion Sīdī Ambar gave them permission to sound all over the bay. It appeared to be a very good harbour to winter in; while that there was sufficient depth was proved by the fact that the <em>Shāhī</em> of Surat, drawing 25 feet, had anchored there. <em>March 17.</em> Sailed from ‘Donda Rogepore’. ‘In the afternoone we arrived in the roade of Dabull, and heere we diserned three junks to be in the porte, which we imagined to be laden for the Red Sea.’ A consultation was held and it was resolved that ‘the <em>Hopewell</em> and <em>Refuge</em> should goe into the port and use their endeavours to bring out the junks if the people should refuse us trade, haveing formerly drawne us thither, to the great charge of our honourable employers, and after put us of with feyned excuses.’ As Hall was the only one thoroughly acquainted with the place, it was settled that he should take in the <em>Hopewell</em>; and Jeronimo de Paiva and Predys went also, to look after the prize goods. The ships displayed a flag of truce but, as soon as they drew near, the forts opened fire upon them. The junks at once set sail; two ran themselves ashore, and the third got so far up the river that the English could not reach her. They managed to get off one of the junks and also a new frigate of some 50 tons; and they might have succeeded in getting the other junk afloat, had not the sailors spent their time pillaging her instead. At midnight they went out again into the road, without any one being hurt, in spite of the heavy fire from the town and forts. ‘This place in my opinion might easely be taken and kept, if we had but people to keepe the castles belonginge thereto; and for a harbour and trade, I think there are fewe better in India.’ <em>March 18.</em> The junk was overhauled, but proved to have little of value on board. The fleet then set sail, Richard Tuke, who had lived among the Portuguese [<em>see</em> p. 197], undertaking to pilot them to ‘Cettora,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-075" href="#sec-08-fn-075" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-075" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> eight leagues to the southward of Dabull, from whence (he tould us) went yearely two junks to the Red Sea’; but he overshot the harbour. <em>March 19.</em></p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-308"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="308" data-folio="254" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n307_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 254">[p. 254]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They anchored at 'Congra' [<em>see</em> p. 255], which Tuke supposed to be 'Seidepore' [<em>see</em> pp. 255, 258], though it was three leagues short of that place. <em>March</em> 20. Off 'Karrapatam'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-076" href="#sec-08-fn-076" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-076" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> they chased two junks, which took refuge in a river.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-077" href="#sec-08-fn-077" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-077" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> They were captured by the boats of the fleet but abandoned on finding that they had nothing on board of any value. <em>March</em> 21. The Governor of 'Karrapatan' offered 9,000 'larees' for the two junks, and this was accepted. A 'cafila' of Portuguese frigates passed. <em>March</em> 20–26. Remained 'heere at Tambona',<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-078" href="#sec-08-fn-078" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-078" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> watering and wooding the fleet. The frigate taken at Dābhol was fitted out to accompany the fleet, while the junk was broken up. As there was no sign of the money being forthcoming for the other two junks, they commenced to unlade them; whereupon the natives made a fresh offer of 4,000 'larrees', 'which we thought better to take then nothing'. 'This port is called Tambona, apperteyning to the Hydalshawe.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-079" href="#sec-08-fn-079" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-079" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> It lyeth in 15 degrees and 40 minutes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-080" href="#sec-08-fn-080" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-080" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> to the northwards of the lyne, being in shew to the eye a very faire harboure. The river runs at least 8 myles up into the country, and yet hath but one place where shipps may winter in, and those of small burthen, as 3 or 400 tonns, which is upon the S. entrance of the harboure. . . . Neere by this place growes store of pepper, which is usually sold for 7, 8 and 9 larees per maund.' <em>March</em> 26. 'As wee were ready to set saile to proceede on our voyage, there came a boate from Karrapatan abord of us, wherein came Signior Thomas de Coste (the Japones<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-081" href="#sec-08-fn-081" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-081" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> which in our former voyage to Dabull came passenger upon our shipps for ditto place) with a message from Sibo Sibo, Avardar,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-082" href="#sec-08-fn-082" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-082" role="doc-noteref">7</a></sup> which is cheife governour <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-309" data-page="309" data-folio="255" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n308_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 255">[p. 255]</button> of a province belonging to the Hidalshawe, to advise us that he would procure us leave to trade in theis partes, and that both of them had written to this effect to the President and Councell at Surratt; and that for the obteyning the Kings ferman this Governours unckle and father in lawe, called by the name of Mammadamy,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-083" href="#sec-08-fn-083" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-083" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> a man in great estimacion with the King, whome he would employ in this busines, and doubted not but to bring us to have trade and commerce with theis people upon good termes, if we could procure a good seguaty [Port. <em>saguatc</em>, a gift] or piscash [Pers. <em>pīshkash</em>, a present] for the King.' A consultation was thereupon held, at which it was decided to go back to 'Seidepore',<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-084" href="#sec-08-fn-084" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-084" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and thence dispatch some factors to 'Rogepore' to treat with the Governor. The messenger was sent back with a demand for a safeconduct and hostages, and with a draft in Portuguese of the concessions required. <em>March 27.</em> The <em>Refuge</em> was dispatched to Batavia [<em>sic</em>]. <em>March 28.</em> Sailed to 'Congra,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-085" href="#sec-08-fn-085" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-085" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> which we supposed to be Seidepore', but found that the latter place was two leagues to leeward. They sounded over the bar, and learnt that the river ran up into the country at least eight or ten miles. <em>March 29.</em> Moved to 'Seidipore'. <em>March 30.</em> 'Signior de Coste came aboard us with two bannian merchants our pledges; from them wee had assurance that the Governour partly did approve of our articles. It was ordered by the Councell of our fleete that myselfe, Signior Jeronimo, and Mr. Turner should goe for Rogepore to treate with Sibo Sibo; and, to creddit our busines and our nation, we carried with us a present for him of theis particulers following: a coat of mayle, vallued at 10<em>l.</em>; two pistolls; a fowleing peece; 16 amberhafted knives; 10 yards of Florence satten. Arriveing at Rogepore, we lodged in the house of Signior Thomas de Coste, where we were visited by all the cheife merchants of the towne and many poore people, which came with small presents, to whom for the honor of our nation <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-310" data-page="310" data-folio="256" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n309_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 256">[p. 256]</button> we extended our liberallitie. The seacond day after our arrivall we were receaved at the Governours house, with all the state the place could affoard. Our present to him was well accepted of, and after he had feasted us, according to the fashion of the Moores, we came to treate with him of our busines. And after some eight or nine houres expence of tyme in handling our articles, and some others by him appointed for us to performe, in case the Portugalls should break the peace betweene them for admitting us leave to trade in those partes, and after he had a while considered with himselfe, he made us this answere: that questionlesse he could procure us the Kings ferman for trade in all the partes and places in the Hydalshawes dominions, but excepted against that article in building a castle, for that, he saied, was hard to be graunted to a strange nation, whose fames made the world to tremble; but tould us, after the King had made some tryall of us and that our factories were established in those partes, peradventure we might obtaine a fort neere the water side for safegaurd of our shipps, or, if that the Portugalls should come and mollest us, theis, said hee, may drawe the King in tyme to graunt your requests; but at your first entrie, he said, by the way of trade, for the King to give away his country he should buy our trade to deare. Farther he tould us it were a principall motive to obtaine the Kings favoure for leave to traffick, to salve the wrongs we had formerly don his subjects, were to present the King with a good seguaty or piscash; and therein rested the conclusion of his speech. To which we made answere: that formerly we had ben twice called and sent for by the Governours of Dabull to come and receave free commerce and trade with them, to the great hinderence and dishonour of our masters, for when we came they put us of with excuses and frivolous delayes, and that our goods could not be transported to and fro but at great charges; soe that for what we tooke of theirs would not satisfie the tenth parte of the charge the[y] put us to in calling us there. We further gave him to understand that those things wee tooke wee sleighted and scorned, and that we gave them to the marriners and never came to our masters stock. We made him knowe alsoe that if we had ben a mallitious people, we could have fired all their junks and vessells in Dabull and other places, and have beaten downe their towne about their eares; and that we had <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-311" data-page="311" data-folio="257" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n310_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 257">[p. 257]</button> noe assurance of our persons and goods, being once ashoare in the King's dominions, without the comaund of a place of our owne. Likewise we tould him that our President at Surratt knew well the customes of the country and what present befitted such a potent king as his master (as he might well judge by our respects with the Mogull and King of Persia), to whome we would refer him. Notwithstanding all theis allegations, and sundry others [which] (fearing to be tedious) I omitt, wee could get noe promise of him for effecting our intentions untill we should promise him what the vallewe of the present should be. Soe we desired him to hould us excused untill we had conferred with our captaine, and that from our shipps wee would resolve him. And soe, after wee had ben freindly entertayned, we parted and came to our shipps.' <em>April</em> 1. A consultation was held on board the <em>Mary</em>, at which it was decided to promise that the present should be of the value of 8,000 rials of eight. The Governor, however, replied that this was too little, and desired that the question should be referred to Surat.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-086" href="#sec-08-fn-086" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-086" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> It was therefore resolved to leave the negotiations to the Presidency. 'I am of opinion, whatsoever it shall cost under 15,000 rials of eight, it is better to come in by theis afore mencioned [the Governor and his uncle] then for us to send unto the King first, and reject their proffer, for in soe doeing we shall purchase their displeasure, whereas we may have them our freinds; and if by their meanes they can procure us a castell, that we may not with the Honourable Companies estate upon all occasions be subject to have a Moores trick put upon us, the mony is well bestowed.' ... 'The harboure of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-312" data-page="312" data-folio="258" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n311_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 258">[p. 258]</button> Seidepore lyeth in the latitude of 16 degrees 12 minutes; being a very good place for shipps to ride in for the esterly monzoone, but noe place for shipps to winter in, by reason of the strong freshes that comes downe the river, which runns soe violent that noe cables can hould them. From the place where we ridd to the towne of Seidipore it is two myles, and the river runns up to the other towne, called Rogepore, 12 miles; and beyond this towne it runns 30 myles into the country. . . . Heere belongeth to the said two townes many junks, which goeth yearely for the Redd Sea, Achin, Combroone, and many other places. Where our shipps ride there is store of fresh water, and at the towne store of refreshing at cheape rates. Heere [we] were promised 5,000 candyes [<em>see</em> p. 245] of pepper per annum, if we had libertie of trade with them. They demaund 9 and 10 larrees per maund, five of theise maunds makeing four at Surratt. The merchants tould us they would yearely fraight us, if we pleased, two shipps for Percia and one to the southward, and would give us, upon all goods sold by the candy, 40 larrees for every candy of goods fraight, and 5 larrees upon every corge of cloath, and to the southward more, as we can agree. Besides pepper, this country affoards indicoes, sugar, gumlack, ginger, turmerick, and all sorts of fine cloathes; all which are bought heere at reasonable prizes, and 40, 50 to 70 per cent. proffitt is usually made from hence to Combroone. Our English commodities are alsoe like to vend heere at good rates, especially corrall, for the most parte of that which wee sell at Surratt is transported by land to Visapore [Bījāpur] (which is the cheife cittie of the Hidalshawes dominions and where he keepes his court), and at divers other places in this kingdome lead, cloath, quicksilver, and ritch stuffes will vend well in theis partes by the relation of theis people; soe doe jewells or any other curious things, farr better then at Surratt.’ <em>April 5 [sic].</em> Letters for Surat were sent to the Governor for conveyance overland. A present of 100 ‘larrees’ was given to Thomas de Coste for his assistance. ‘Since our being heere in the port of Seidypore, Captaine Hall, Mr. Evans, and Mr. Pashley have ben at Karrapotan, which is one league to the southward of this place. By their relacion it is the best harboure in all India, for 100 saile of shipps may winter there safely. From the mouth of the entry of the river to the towne of Karrapatan it is at least 16 myles up ....</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-313"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="313" data-folio="259" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n312_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 259">[p. 259]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">It is an excellent good place alsoe for fortificacion, and good store of fresh water by the rivers mouth; where alsoe lyeth great stoare of stones already hewen out. Soe that heere wanteth nothing but the Kings leave; then a small matter would build a fort to oppose any enymie.' <em>April</em> 5. The ships sailed. 'If the Companie make a newe joynt stock, and that heere be good supplies of moneys and commodities out of England, the shipps may retorne for England laden the same yeare they come out (if not all, most of them), and make their voyage in lesse then 20 moneths, as well as the Portugalls. Alsoe if by our trade the Portugall break with this king, as it is hoped he will, wee shall hinder the Portugalls from 5 or 6,000 saylors, which he hath upon all occasions out of the Hidalshawes dominions, that rowes in his friggotts for small meanes.' <em>April</em> 6. 'In the morning wee were within a league of the barre of Goa, where we diserned three castles; one upon the north side, a very bigg place upon the top of a high hill, where is a round platforme like a lanthorne, envyroned round with walls which reacheth downe to the waterside. Another castle you shall finde upon the south side, opposite to the former; and another built lately upon the northermost end of one of the Iles of Keymathes, which is the first iland neerest Goa.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-087" href="#sec-08-fn-087" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-087" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Two or three ilands more are neere adjoyning, all of them called by the name of Burnt Ilands, and indeede the places brooketh the name very well, for there is nothing upon them. From under some of theis ilands came out unto us 12 man a warre friggotts, at whom the <em>Starre</em>, being asterne of the fleete and neerest them, made some 8 or 9 shott at them, and then they left us. Theis friggotts were built purposely to keepe the barre upon all occasions, to rescue any of their shipps that shall come upon the coast in distresse. The money that paid for them was by collection of the peoples freewill at Goa, and called the Caffila de Calleta.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-088" href="#sec-08-fn-088" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-088" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>April</em> 7. Reached the island of 'Angedive'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-089" href="#sec-08-fn-089" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-089" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> They noticed two <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-314" data-page="314" data-folio="260" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n313_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 260">[p. 260]</button> platforms, one at the north and the other at the south end of the island. ‘In former tymes carricks have careened in this place and wintred, and it is supposed by us that those platformes . . . . were made by the Portugalls.’ <em>April</em> 8. ‘Wee woodded and ballasted our shipps and went ashoare upon the maine. Heere was brought us store of henns and fruite, but we could get noe beeves, by reason that the governour of the place lived about half a dayes journey from the water side, and in his absense, the inhabitants tould us, they durst not sell any. Neere by this place growes great store of pepper and ginger; and here is to be had many other commodities, as in the Hydalxa his country, to whome this continent alsoe belongeth. This country is alsoe full of tymber to build, and not passing three leagues from this place is a river from whence the Portugalls have a great parte of the wood wherewith they build their vessells at Goa. This place might easely be fortified and kept from any enymie (especially upon the iland) without any great mollestation, for from the iland you may shoote over to the maine, and if we had any ordinaunce upon theis platformes our shipps might securely ride under their shelter. Besides, if we had this place under our comaund wee should annoy the Portugalls very much, in keepeing from him all his masts that cometh from Cannanore to Goa, for they towe them betweene this iland and the maine. Further, this place lyeth in the heart of India for trade with the Mallabarrs, Callicutts, and Cochinders, which places abound with pepper, all which we should in tyme, either by faire termes or constraint, make them bring their commodities thither, or else admitt us leave to trade in their country. All the difficultie that I conceave may be prejudiciall to our masters is this: the distance of the way betweene this and Surratt, which wilbe a long tyme ere our shipps can turn it upp, and very dangerous for a lone shipp to passe by Goa, our enymies port. But in coming from Surratt hither there is noe danger.’ <em>April</em> 9. Passed the castle of ‘Onor’ [Honāwar], which report said was garrisoned by only twelve Portuguese and some blacks. <em>April</em> 10. Reaching Mangalore, they found two junks in the bay. At first Captain Hall thought of sending his boats to fetch them out, but on consideration it was decided not to lose time by attempting this. Passing along the coast two more junks were seen and captured. <em>April</em> 11. The prizes were overhauled <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-315" data-page="315" data-folio="261" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n314_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 261">[p. 261]</button> and found to be empty. A Malabar junk was chased ashore and some goods taken out of her. In the afternoon another junk was captured and taken into ‘Mountdelee’ [<em>see</em> p. 125]. <em>April</em> 12. A message from the King of Cannanore promised supplies if the fleet would come thither, but hearing that there were at Cochin three ships from Ceylon, bound for Goa, it was decided to make for that port without delay. The three junks were left empty at anchor, and a letter was written to the King of Cannanore, stating that they would accept his offer of trade on some future occasion. In sailing along the coast, the <em>Hart's</em> barge attacked a frigate, although Captain Hall had ordered that no more of these poor country vessels should be meddled with. However, she made so stout a resistance that James Cheshire, master of the barge, was killed and eleven men hurt. <em>April</em> 15. They reached Cochin, where they saw the three ships in the road and seven or eight others further in. They were also thirteen frigates at anchor near the bar. The seamen objected to make an attack, as the water was found to be shallow and bad weather was feared; so the project was abandoned. ‘The towne of Cochin is almost as great as Goa, and fairely built with stone; wherein are many churches and castles. A mile and a half up the river stands the Kings towne, from whence he is able to put in feild 5,000 blacks in armes according to their manner, in the behalfe of the Portugall and himselfe. Neverthelesse, this place is easeye to be taken if we had three or four shipps like the <em>Hopewell</em>, with a pilote to conduct them into the harboure; for theis friggotts we mett with were heere by chance to conduct a caffila for Goa.’ <em>April</em> 19. Saw the high land of ‘Brian John’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-090" href="#sec-08-fn-090" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-090" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> ‘This was the last land wee sawe upon the coast of India.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-091" href="#sec-08-fn-091" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-091" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>April</em> 25. Crossed the Line. <em>May</em> 23. Sighted Rodriguez. <em>May</em> 27. Reached Mauritius. <em>May</em> 28. Got into a harbour there, where they obtained plenty of refreshments. <em>June</em> 7. The <em>William</em> arrived. <em>June</em> 17. Two men punished for a detestable crime. (<em>Last entry.</em> 26¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-316"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="316" data-folio="262" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n315_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 262">[p. 262]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account, by David Davis in the</span> <em>HART</em> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xliv. p. 67).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 1. ‘The President Keeredge came to Swaley to hasten our ships away to sea; but that night (as God would have it) the Portingalls trechery was found out by Jeronimo.’ <em>March</em> 3. ‘The Portugall was examined, who confessed the plott that was pretended against us at Bombay; whereuppon he had the topp of his fingers burnt and the topp of his nose. The plott was that we should come to Bombay and take a castle peaceably, which castell Captain Hale had beene before with the <em>Mary</em>, <em>Hopewell</em>, and <em>Refuge</em> the while [we?], viz. the <em>Exchange</em>, the <em>William</em>, the <em>Hart</em>, and the <em>Starr</em>, were at Persia; and after that we had landed our men and had the possession thereof, that there was a vault under the castell full of powder ready to blow us upp. Then that 5,000 souldiors should come from the maine in theyr frigotts and enter our ships and cutt of all our heads that should be left.’ <em>March</em> 12. The fleet, consisting of the <em>Mary</em>, <em>Hart</em>, <em>Star</em>, <em>Hopewell</em>, and <em>Refuge</em>, sailed from Swally, leaving there the <em>Exchange</em> and the <em>William</em> and five Dutch ships. <em>March</em> 15. ‘We were before the entring in at Danderagpore, which is a good harbour within a deepe bay, and a maine strong castell, which is the Dickerny [Dakhinī] countrey.’ <em>March</em> 16. ‘Our barge went in and sounded about the castell.’ <em>March</em> 17. ‘We weighed and stood out of the bay.’ <em>March</em> 18. Sailed from the Road [of Dābhol]. <em>March</em> 20. The <em>Hopewell</em> chased two Malabar junks into ‘Tomanars’, where they were captured and proved to be laden with coco-nuts, betel-nuts, and cardamomum seeds. <em>March</em> 21. The Governor agreed to purchase the prizes for 9,000 ‘larris’, equivalent to 7,200 mahmūdīs or 360<em>l. March</em> 26. Received 4,000 ‘laris’ for the two junks. <em>March</em> 27. The <em>Refuge</em> departed for Batavia. <em>March</em> 28. The remaining vessels sailed northwards again. <em>March</em> 29. Anchored at ‘Radgpoore’. <em>April</em> 4. Sailed. Called at ‘Carripotame’, where the barges went up for six miles. <em>April</em> 7. Reached the island of ‘Angeduo’. <em>April</em> 8. Left, after purchasing provisions, and sailed southwards. <em>April</em> 11. Anchored at ‘the roade of Mount Deley’ with three prize junks. <em>April</em> 12. Sailed again. <em>May</em> 23. Saw an island, which proved to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-317" data-page="317" data-folio="263" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n316_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 263">[p. 263]</button> be ‘Deago de Roys’ [Rodriguez]. <em>May</em> 27. Sighted Mauritius. <em>May</em> 29. Anchored there. <em>June</em> 7. The <em>William</em> came in. <em>June</em> 18. The whole fleet sailed, after being detained some hours by Kerridge and the commanders being on shore hunting. <em>June</em> 21. Reached ‘Englands Forrest’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-092" href="#sec-08-fn-092" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-092" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>June</em> 26. The <em>William</em> parted company for England. <em>July</em> 1. Saw Madagascar. <em>July</em> 6. Anchored in the road at St. Augustine’s, and found there the <em>Eagle</em>, which had been beaten back from the Cape. <em>July</em> 11. Sailed again. <em>July</em> 19. Saw the African coast. <em>July</em> 20. Three boats were sent to take a Portuguese frigate, but were repulsed with eight men wounded. <em>July</em> 22. Anchored in Mozambique Road, where they could descry four sails in the harbour. <em>July</em> 23. ‘Our commanders went ashore with the five ships boats and neere 100 shott, and landed on the point of the headland of the maine and marched som two miles upp into the land, where nobody resisted.’ <em>July</em> 31. The fleet sailed. <em>August</em> 3. Saw the Comoros. <em>August</em> 4. Anchored at Johanna. <em>August</em> 20. Went to Mohilla, but finding no ships there proceeded for Surat. <em>September</em> 27. Saw the Indian coast near Bassein. <em>September</em> 30. Reached Swally, and found there the <em>Jonas</em>, <em>Expedition</em>, and a junk taken by the former; also four Dutch ships. <em>October</em> 4. Came into the Hole the <em>Exchange</em>, the <em>Blessing</em>, the <em>Christopher</em>, and the <em>Blessing's</em> prize, named the <em>Little James</em>; also two Dutch ships from the Red Sea. (19 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Another Account, by John Pashley, Master of the</span> <em>STAR</em> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. xlviii. p. 53).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 12. The fleet sailed. <em>March</em> 15. Anchored near ‘a castell called Danda. This castell standeth one a small iland aboute pistall shott from the maine’. The boats sounded the harbour for some distance. <em>March</em> 17. Set sail. In the afternoon anchored in Dābhol Road. Captain Hall with the <em>Hopewell</em> and <em>Refuge</em> went into the river and captured a junk and a frigate ‘in despighte of the towne’. <em>March</em> 18. Captain Hall got out of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-318" data-page="318" data-folio="264" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n317_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 264">[p. 264]</button> the river, with the loss of one or two men wounded, although he had to run the gauntlet of three or four forts. The fleet sailed. <em>March</em> 20. Anchored off a port called ‘Tamana’. <em>March</em> 21. Seventy or eighty Portuguese frigates, bound to the northwards, came by. <em>March</em> 25. On receiving a message from the Governor of ‘Ragapore’, it was resolved to proceed thither. <em>March</em> 27. The <em>Refuge</em> was speeded to Batavia. <em>March</em> 29. The ships reached the port of Rājāpur. <em>March</em> 30. Hostages having been received, the English merchants started to visit the Governor. <em>April</em> 2. The merchants returned with proposals from him. An answer was sent by two Portuguese. <em>April</em> 4. The ships sailed. The <em>Star</em> and the <em>Hopewell</em> went into the port of ‘Caropotan’ to survey the harbour. The Portuguese returned with an answer from the Governor of Rājāpur. <em>April</em> 5. The fleet sailed. <em>April</em> 6. Stood into Goa Road, but found only frigates there. <em>April</em> 7. Anchored at the island of ‘Angydue’. <em>April</em> 8. Left again. <em>April</em> 10. Near Mangalore two small junks were captured. <em>April</em> 11. A third was picked up, and the fleet anchored ‘under Mountdelee’. <em>April</em> 12. Sailed for Cochin. <em>April</em> 15. Reached that place, and fired at a squadron of frigates lying there; but finding that no good could be done the fleet sailed again. <em>April</em> 17. Saw ‘the towne of Colane [Quilon], the which allsoe is inhabeted by the Portugall’. <em>April</em> 25. Crossed the Line. <em>May</em> 24. Captain Hall attempted to land at the island of Rodriguez, but the surf was too violent; so the voyage was resumed. <em>May</em> 28. Anchored in a harbour at the island of Mauritius. <em>June</em> 7. The <em>William</em> arrived. <em>June</em> 18. They all set sail. <em>June</em> 21. Sighted the island of Réunion [<em>see</em> p. 263]. ‘Wee would have ankered at this iland, but found noe convenient place.’ <em>June</em> 26. The <em>William</em> parted company. <em>July</em> 1. Saw land. <em>July</em> 6. Anchored at St. Augustine’s, where the <em>Eagle</em> was found riding. She was homewards bound from Bantam, and had got as far as the Cape but was beaten back by bad weather. She was leaky, and her crew were sickly, seventeen having died already. <em>July</em> 7. The <em>Eagle</em> was pronounced unfit to proceed to England; and so it was decided to repair her and carry her to Surat. <em>July</em> 9. On the complaint of his crew, Eustace Man was removed from the command of the <em>Eagle</em> and sent a prisoner on board the <em>Hart</em>. Peter <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-319" data-page="319" data-folio="265" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n318_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 265">[p. 265]</button> Cowlan was made master in his place. A brisk trade was carried on with the natives for cattle and sheep in exchange for red beads. <em>July</em> 11. The fleet sailed. <em>July</em> 19. Saw the coast of Mozambique. The ships spread themselves in a long line to seawards, in order to intercept any Portuguese vessels that might be passing. <em>July</em> 20. The boats attacked a frigate, but returned with four or five men hurt. <em>July</em> 22. Anchored off Mozambique, where five small Portuguese vessels were riding. <em>July</em> 23. Two or three hundred men landed ‘on Cabasera’ [Cape Cabeceira, on the north side of Mozambique approach] and marched a mile or two to some Portuguese houses. The inhabitants fled; and the sailors returned with some oranges and lemons and nineteen ‘negers’. <em>July</em> 24. The <em>Hopewell</em> and the <em>John</em> were sent to sea to look for Portuguese vessels. <em>July</em> 26. They returned. A boat was captured, which had been dispatched to give warning of the English being there. <em>July</em> 31. The fleet sailed. <em>August</em> 4. Anchored at Johanna. <em>August</em> 5. The cargo of the <em>Eagle</em> was ordered to be transferred to the <em>Star</em>. <em>August</em> 7. The <em>Hopewell</em> was sent to Mohilla to look for the expected fleet from England. <em>August</em> 15. She returned. <em>August</em> 20. The fleet stood over to Mohilla, but found no ships there. The voyage was accordingly resumed. <em>September</em> 27. Saw the coast of India. <em>September</em> 30. Anchored in Swally Road, where they found the <em>Jonas</em> and the <em>Expedition</em>. <em>October</em> 1. Went into the Hole. Four Dutch ships from Batavia were there. <em>October</em> 5. The <em>Exchange</em> and two ‘Flemings’ arrived from the Red Sea; also the <em>Blessing</em>, the <em>Christopher</em>, and the pinnace <em>Coco-nut</em> (a prize of the <em>Blessing</em>) from Bantam. <em>October</em> 11. A drunken Dutchman stabbed an Englishman, causing his death; two others were hurt [<em>see</em> p. 292]. <em>October</em> 12. A Dutch ship arrived from Batavia. <em>October</em> 15. Thomas Robinson was sent on board as a prisoner. <em>October</em> 20. The trial of Eustace Man was commenced. <em>November</em> 11. The <em>Royal Mary</em> and the pinnace <em>John</em> sailed for Bantam <em>December</em> 11. ‘The pinis <em>James</em> (a Portugall prise which was taken by the <em>Blessing</em> goinge to Bantom), shee haveing bin upe Surrat Ryver to bee trimmed, and comminge downe againe with two barges to atend her, was by 16 Portugall frygots fyred, most of the men slaine and taken prisoners by the Portugalles. Som escaped in the two barges and runn one shore, but the Portugalls <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-320" data-page="320" data-folio="266" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n319_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 266">[p. 266]</button> persuinge them surprised the barges. They beelonged, one to the <em>Harte</em>, the other to our shipp the <em>Starr</em>; and of our men wee lost five eyther slaine or taken prisoners.' (31 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Another Account, by Abraham Sayers in the</span> <em>HOPEWELL</em> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xlvi. p. 36).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 12. Sailed. <em>March</em> 15. Arrived at 'Donderogerpoore'. <em>March</em> 17. Left again, and anchored at Dābhol. <em>March</em> 18. Captain Hall took two prizes. <em>March</em> 20. Two small junks captured at 'Tamena'. <em>March</em> 28. Came away from 'Apagode,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-093" href="#sec-08-fn-093" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-093" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> otherwise called Tamena by the people of the country'. <em>March</em> 30. Anchored at 'Nossapora', two leagues from 'Carrapotan'. <em>April</em> 4. Moved to the latter place. <em>April</em> 5. Sailed. <em>April</em> 7. Came to 'Angedue'. <em>April</em> 9. Departed. <em>April</em> 11. Reached 'Mountadele'. <em>April</em> 12. Left again. <em>May</em> 24. Visited Rodriguez, but could not find a place to anchor. <em>May</em> 27. Reached 'the Morrosses' [Mauritius]. <em>May</em> 28. Went into the harbour. <em>June</em> 18. The <em>Mary</em>, <em>Hart</em>, <em>Star</em>, <em>Hopewell</em>, and a frigate, sailed. The <em>William</em>, which left Swally a month after the fleet, homewards bound, accompanied them. <em>June</em> 21. Saw the island of 'Domas Carenus' [Do Mascarenhas: <em>see</em> p. 263]. <em>June</em> 22. Departed again. <em>June</em> 26. In lat. 25° 36' the <em>William</em> quitted them. <em>July</em> 1. Saw the coast of Madagascar. <em>July</em> 6. Anchored at St. Augustine's, finding there the <em>Eagle</em>, bound for England. <em>July</em> 11. Sailed again. <em>July</em> 18. Reached the headland called 'Doe Tangars', about 25 leagues from Mozambique. Resolved that the fleet should lie off and on to wait for 'a carreck that comes to Mousambege every yeare'. 'We did chase a Portingale fregat with three barges, and sett her up and fought withe her and had 5 men spoyld and were forcd to put off from her, in regard it was night and the ships a great way from us.' <em>July</em> 21. Bore up for Mozambique. <em>July</em> 22. Anchored in the Road. <em>July</em> 31. Sailed again. <em>August</em> 4. Anchored at 'St. John' [Johanna]. <em>August</em> 20. Departed. <em>September</em> 27. Saw the Indian coast. <em>September</em> 30. Reached Swally. The <em>Jonas</em> had arrived two or three days earlier. (19 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-321"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="321" data-folio="267" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n320_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 267">[p. 267]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account, by Edward Austin in the</span> <em>HART</em> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. xlvii. p. 38).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 12. The fleet sailed. <em>March</em> 15. Reached Danda-Rājpurī. <em>March</em> 17. Anchored at Dābhol. Captain Hall went into the harbour with the <em>Hopewell</em> and <em>Refuge</em>, and brought out a junk and a frigate, after firing a hundred shots at the town. <em>March</em> 18. Sailed. <em>March</em> 20. The <em>Hopewell</em> chased two junks into ‘Tambona’. <em>March</em> 27. The <em>Refuge</em> left for Batavia.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-094" href="#sec-08-fn-094" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-094" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>March</em> 28. The fleet sailed. <em>March</em> 29. Reached ‘Rashapore’. <em>April</em> 4. The <em>Star</em> and the <em>Hopewell</em> went to ‘Callepatana’ for water. <em>April</em> 5. Sailed. <em>April</em> 6. Were off Goa. <em>April</em> 7. Anchored at ‘Angidue’. <em>April</em> 10. Off Mangalore took two junks with coco-nuts. <em>April</em> 11. Captured another junk and anchored at ‘Mountalee’. <em>April</em> 14. Anchored two leagues from Cochin. <em>April</em> 15. Fired at a squadron of thirteen frigates. <em>May</em> 23. Saw ‘the Degorayes’ [Rodriguez]. <em>May</em> 28. Reached Mauritius. <em>June</em> 7. The <em>William</em> came in. <em>June</em> 18. The fleet sailed. <em>June</em> 21. Passed ‘the English Forrest’ [<em>see</em> p. 263]. <em>June</em> 27. The <em>William</em> departed for England. <em>July</em> 1. Sighted Madagascar. <em>July</em> 6. Anchored in ‘Augustin Bay’. <em>July</em> 11. Set sail, in company with the <em>Eagle</em>, which was in the road when they arrived. <em>July</em> 19. Reached the African coast. <em>July</em> 20. The ships’ boats attacked a frigate, but were beaten off, with six men hurt. <em>July</em> 22. Anchored off Mozambique. <em>July</em> 23. The boats went ashore and came back with some oranges and lemons and 12 or 16 negro prisoners. <em>July</em> 24. The <em>Hopewell</em> and the pinnace <em>John</em> were sent to look for vessels. <em>July</em> 25. They chased a boat ashore. <em>July</em> 31. The fleet sailed. <em>August</em> 4. Anchored at Johanna. <em>August</em> 20. Stood over to Mohilla (the <em>Eagle</em> still in company). Sailed for Surat. <em>September</em> 27. ‘Fell with the land of St. Johns.’ <em>September</em> 30. Anchored at Swally Bar, and found there the <em>Jonas</em> and <em>Expedition</em>, newly arrived from England. <em>October</em> 2. Went into the Hole, where four Dutch ships were riding. <em>October</em> 4. Arrived the <em>Blessing</em>, the <em>Christopher</em>, and a small frigate, from Bantam; also the <em>Exchange</em> and two Hollanders from the Red Sea. (20¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-322"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="322" data-folio="268" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n321_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 268">[p. 268]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account</span>, <span class="sc">by Peter Andrews</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-095" href="#sec-08-fn-095" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-095" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xlix. p. 24).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>March</em> 12. Sailed. <em>March</em> 15. Anchored at 'Danda'. <em>March</em> 17. Sailed to Dābhol. <em>March</em> 18. Having made two prizes, they departed. <em>March</em> 19. Anchored at 'Gouncore' [<em>see</em> p. 255]. <em>March</em> 20. Went into 'Tambona'. <em>March</em> 24. A boat came from 'Ragapore', with a 'Portingall' (who 'came passenger with us the voyage before to Dabull' [<em>see</em> p. 254]), inviting the English to come and trade at that place; whereupon it was decided that the <em>Mary</em>, <em>Hart</em>, and <em>Star</em> should go thither, leaving the <em>Hopewell</em>, <em>Refuge</em>, and the frigate behind. <em>March</em> 27. The <em>Refuge</em> started for Batavia. <em>March</em> 28. The three ships sailed but overshot their destination and anchored at 'Gauncore' once more. <em>March</em> 29. Reached 'Ragapore' [Jaitāpur]. 'This baye is a most dainty bay to ride in ... and is a most daynty place for all manner of fishe, and for oysters no better in the world. The people used us very kindly.' <em>April</em> 4. Moved to 'Carrapotan', where the <em>Star</em> and <em>Hopewell</em> explored the bay 'and fownd it a most brave harbour'. <em>April</em> 5. The fleet sailed. <em>April</em> 6. Passed Goa. <em>April</em> 7. Anchored at the island of 'Angudua'. <em>April</em> 8. Set sail. <em>April</em> 10. Reached Mangalore; sailed again and captured two junks. <em>April</em> 11. Took a third junk, and went in under 'Mount Dillee'. <em>April</em> 12. Departed. <em>April</em> 14. Anchored at Cochin. <em>April</em> 15. Fired at a squadron of frigates, 'but they rewe awaye'. Resumed the voyage. <em>April</em> 16. 'Strock downe 20 peece of ordnance'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-096" href="#sec-08-fn-096" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-096" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>May</em> 23. Saw 'Digoe Royze'. <em>May</em> 24. Endeavoured to land, but could not do so on account of the surf. <em>May</em> 27. Sighted Mauritius. <em>May</em> 28. Warped into a harbour there. <em>June</em> 7. The <em>William</em> anchored 'thwart of Turtle Baye'. <em>June</em> 18. Sailed. They had careened and ballasted the <em>Mary</em>, and taken in water and provisions. <em>June</em> 26. The <em>William</em> departed. <em>July</em> 1. Saw the southern coast of Madagascar. <em>July</em> 6. Reached 'Augusteene Bay', where they found the <em>Eagle</em>. <em>July</em> 10. Andrews was made master of the <em>Hart</em>. <em>July</em> 11. The fleet sailed. <em>July</em> 19. Saw land. <em>July</em> 20. Chased a vessel, which proved to be <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-323" data-page="323" data-folio="269" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n322_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 269">[p. 269]</button> ‘a great Portingall friggott and full of menn and well appointed’. The <em>Hart's</em> barge made an attack, but, as the other boats failed to support her, she was beaten off with seven men badly hurt. <em>July</em> 22. Came into Mozambique Road. <em>July</em> 23. A force was landed, which burnt some Portuguese houses and brought back twenty slaves. <em>July</em> 25. The <em>Hopewell</em> returned, with a captured Portuguese boat. <em>July</em> 31. The fleet sailed for the Comoro Islands. <em>August</em> 4. Anchored at Johanna. <em>August</em> 7. The <em>Star</em> began to take in the <em>Eagle's</em> lading; and the <em>Hopewell</em> went to Mohilla. <em>August</em> 20. The fleet departed. <em>September</em> 27. Sighted the coast of India. <em>September</em> 30. Reached the Bar of Swally, where the <em>Jonas</em> and <em>Expedition</em> were riding. There were four Dutch ships in the Hole. <em>October</em> 3. The <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Exchange</em>, <em>Christopher</em>, and two Hollanders came in sight. <em>November</em> 11. The <em>Mary</em> sailed for Bantam. <em>November</em> 18. The <em>Hart</em> began to lade for England. <em>November</em> 25. Three Dutch ships sailed for Batavia. <em>December</em> 8. The <em>Hart's</em> destination was changed to Persia. <em>December</em> 12. A prize burnt by the Portuguese frigates, with the loss of about thirty men. (21¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Christopher Brown at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 11, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 558).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Is sorry to hear they must miss the Agra caravan. Captain Hall had departed before the letter arrived. (<em>Copy.</em> ¼ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin, Nathaniel West, and Jeremy Shuker at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 12, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 558).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received the 6,000 mahmūdīs; also 1,490 from the Governor for sheet lead sold to him. The goods were cleared on the 10th, and yesterday the Ahmadābād caravan was sent on to Surat, carrying also some cotton yarn and cloth from this place. Yesterday arrived a second consignment from Ahmadābād, in company with the goods of the Dutch; will forward them as soon as possible. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received March</em> 13.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-324"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="324" data-folio="270" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n323_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 270">[p. 270]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Mountney, Nathaniel Wyche, and William Fremlen at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, March 16, 1628</span> (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 562).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Nothing heard yet of the goods sent from Surat for transmission to Agra; but Hopkinson, who left last Tuesday, met them two days ago on the other side of the river, and so they will be here shortly. Have remitted Rs. 10,000 to Agra by exchange, and hope to send a similar amount shortly. The exchange is very high, ‘being 97 for 100.’ Cannot get rid of the ‘sunneeas’ [<em>see</em> p. 235] sent up, except at a loss; they cost 30⅛ mahmūdīs in Surat, but here are only worth Rs. 12¾, equivalent to 28¾ mahmūdīs. The necessity of getting funds to pay their sugar merchants, ‘being banjarres,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-097" href="#sec-08-fn-097" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-097" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and therefore cannot stay for their monies,’ has induced them to pledge the gold for Rs. 8,000, at one-half per cent. interest. Offer for lead in exchange for indigo; request instructions. The ‘rowtee’ [Hind. <em>rāotī</em>, a small tent] will be brought down by Tottle and the rest. <em>P.S.</em>—Report a further offer for the lead. (<em>Copy. 2¼ pp. Received March 21.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Gregory Clement, John Bangham, and Ralph Cartwright at Agra to the Same, March</span> 17, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 580).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan has now been dispatched, but only after much trouble, first with their creditors and the camelmen, and then with the King, for buying and transporting saltpetre without authority. ‘Gregory Clement and John Bangham weare kept a whole daie and night prisoners in the castle. At last, after enquirie, the King gave licence; and wee, giving a bribe and present to his mynion, Reza Bauder,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-098" href="#sec-08-fn-098" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-098" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> weare released, and our caphila had leave to departe, and moreover obteyned licence for the buying of 2,000 m[au]ns more. The Dutch hath been troubled concerning saltpeter in like manner.’ The caravan started on the 11th. Invoice now forwarded. Rs. 2,000 <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-325" data-page="325" data-folio="271" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n324_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 271">[p. 271]</button> advanced to Bangham for charges on the way. Had hopes of selling Abbot's emeralds to Mīr Mūsā, but he has returned them. He recently lent the factors Rs.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-099" href="#sec-08-fn-099" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-099" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> 1,500, which proved very useful. On the strength of Vīrjī Vorā's letter of credit they succeeded in borrowing Rs. 5,000, and 'Bimgee' guaranteed a loan of Rs. 3,000 for them. They have had the greatest trouble for want of means to clear their caravan, and hope never to undergo the like. Estate of the late Justinian Offley. His cousin, Robert Clitherow, privately conveyed away a bill of debt of his own for 200<em>l.</em>, as acknowledged in a note quoted at length. Refer also to a consultation held here on December 31, in which Clitherow's demeanour, carriage, and proceedings were censured. Send 'the Kings laskars accompt', as it stands in their books. Bangham forwards a journal and ledger, and desires that they may be dispatched to England in lieu of those already sent down. 'Since our last wee vissited the Nabob Assuffe Caun, to whome wee had frendlie accesse, himselfe promising his favour and furtherance in all our occasions. Yesterdaie hee feasted the King sumptuouslie att his owne house. The present occurrences in court is a gennerall lessening of former livings and mayntenance of all degrees of the late Kings amraws and servants, with dispeed of divers of them to sundrie parts of theis dominions.' <em>P.S.</em>—Bangham has been detained here for various purposes, but leaves to-morrow to overtake the caravan. Muqarrab Khān still delays the settlement of his account. (<em>Copy.</em> 3½ <em>pp. Received April 5.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Gregory Clement and Ralph Cartwright at Agra to the Same, March</span> 17, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 584).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Eight fardles of saltpetre left behind, owing to the camels falling into a well and breaking their necks. Have recovered the camelhire, and will keep the saltpetre till the next caravan. Request two crooked sword-blades for a friend at court; also two or three handsome cases of strong waters, which 'wilbee verry acceptable to divers of the amraws here, especiallie Assaphe Caun'. (<em>Copy. ½ p. Received April 5.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-326"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="326" data-folio="272" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n325_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 272">[p. 272]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Matthew Morton at Swally to the President at Surat, March</span> 17, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 559).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Assures him that he is not unmindful of the fate of the <em>Whale</em>. Will not take in any light goods, as tobacco and cotton wool, till the Company's rice has been received, and then the ship will be nearly full. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same, March</span> 18, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 560).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The ship has its water and wood aboard, and by to-night will take in the Moors' cloth. She will then be full; and he urges that either the President or Mr. Wylde should come down and dispatch them. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Clitherow at 'Antree'</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-100" href="#sec-08-fn-100" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-100" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">to the Same, March</span> 18, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 579).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Last night, being at Gwalior with the caravan, he received their letters of February 25, which, after perusal, he sent on to Agra. The caravan consists of 261 camels, laden with indigo and saltpetre. It cannot now be at Surat in time to catch the ships for England. Goodwin accompanies him, Bangham having stayed behind for reasons best known to himself. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received April</em> 2.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin and Jeremy Shuker at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 20, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 560).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Martin hopes to be at Surat in five or six days. Send an account of money disbursed in clearing the goods. The letter has been forwarded to Baroda. <em>Annexed.</em>—The account referred to.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-101" href="#sec-08-fn-101" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-101" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (<em>Copy.</em> 2¼ <em>pp. Received March</em> 21.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-327"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="327" data-folio="273" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n326_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 273">[p. 273]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Richard Barber at Baroda to the Same, March</span> 20, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 570).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yesterday speeded 14 carts, laden with 52 bales of goods. Southaike will accompany them with a guard. Encloses the invoice. (<em>Copy.  <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> p.  Received March</em> 23.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Robert Clitherow and John Goodwin at 'Nerrer'</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-102" href="#sec-08-fn-102" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-102" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <span class="sc">to the Same, March</span> 21, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 584).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The caravan has reached this place in safety. Bangham has not yet overtaken them. The King's farmān 'is in divers places obeyed, and in some not regarded, in which places wee are forced to paie as they please'. (<em>Copy.  <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> p.  Received April</em> 6.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Mountney, Nathaniel Wyche, and William Fremlen at Ahmadābād to the Same, March</span> 21, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 573).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Tāj Khān has promised his utmost assistance in remitting money to Agra. Sent thither bills for Rs. 10,000 eight days ago, and hope soon to make another remittance of the same amount. At present the exchange is very high; 'they will give but 100 for 97 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub>.' If Mr. Barker's carpets are not bought by the Governor, they shall be sent with the jewels, &amp;c., to Agra, along with the other goods brought by Robert Tottle. 'Mirza Muckey', the Dīwān, is bound thither in five or six days, and they propose to let them proceed in his company. Yesterday arrived 'Cojah Jehaun' [Khwāja Jahān], who was Dīwān formerly and is now appointed to succeed to that office. Will let Tottle have their old bay horse, though he is scarcely fit for service; and will send the other one along with him. Have concluded the bargain for indigo in exchange for lead. <em>P.S.</em>— The quicksilver has been delivered to Tāj Khān. (<em>Copy.  1 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> pp. Received March</em> 26.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-328"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="328" data-folio="274" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n327_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 274">[p. 274]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Christopher Brown</span>, aboard the <em>WILLIAM</em> at Swally, to the President at Surat, March 24, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 571).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Fears that they cannot find room for the cotton yarn, as the silk occupies more space than was expected. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Martin</span>, <span class="sc">Nathaniel West</span>, and <span class="sc">Jeremy Shuker</span> at Broach to the President and Council at Surat, March 25, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 571).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Yesterday sent off the Baroda goods, and some of their own. Enclose an invoice for the latter. The Ahmadābād goods were dispeeded over the river to-day, ‘but customed att randome,’ as the particulars were not accurately known, owing to want of advice from the factors there. A parcel of fine blue baftas sent in the care of Southaike. As directed, Martin will remain three or four days longer, by which time they hope the rest of their goods will be ready. <em>P.S.</em>—Will advise later the charges on these goods. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received March</em> 26.)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Malachi Martin</span> at Swally to the President at Surat, March 25, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 575).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The two country boats dismissed, their services not being needed at present. Goods shipped on board the <em>William</em>. Cartage of the lead. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><span class="sc">Captain John Hall</span>, <span class="sc">Richard Predys</span>, <span class="sc">Andrew Evans</span>, <span class="sc">Peter Elliott</span>, <span class="sc">John Pashley</span>, and <span class="sc">Daniel White</span>, aboard the <em>MARY</em> in ‘Tamona’ Road, to the President and Council at Bantam, March 27, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Java</em>, vol. iii. pt. ii. p. 457).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">In accordance with instructions from Surat they now dispeed this ship, the <em>Refuge</em>, with letters, &amp;c. Narrate their proceedings at Dābhol. The rice found in the captured junk has been put on board the <em>Refuge</em>. Two Malabar junks chased into ‘this poart of Tamana’ [<em>see</em> p. 254], taken, and sold. Yesterday a messenger came from ‘Sibbo Sibo, chief governer of all the poorts belonging to the Lydall Shaw’ [<em>sic</em>], offering to procure the King’s farmān for trade <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-329" data-page="329" data-folio="275" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n328_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 275">[p. 275]</button> if they would settle a factory at ‘Carap[atan]’, and promising to supply 2,000 tons of pepper yearly at 9 or 10 ‘lareis’ per maund. Have accordingly sent him some draft articles of agreement (copy enclosed), and intend to wait four or five days for his reply. As the letters from Surat may be urgent, they have thought it best to dispatch the <em>Refuge</em> at once. (<em>Copy.</em> 2½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Kerridge, Richard Wylde, John Skibbow, and George Page at Surat to the Same, March</span> 28, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 456).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-103" href="#sec-08-fn-103" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-103" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Enclose a copy of their last letter, dated March 8, sent by the pinnace <em>Refuge</em>. Now forward the blue calicoes therein promised. The <em>Exchange</em> sailed on March 22 for Mokha, and was followed within an hour by two Dutch ships.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-104" href="#sec-08-fn-104" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-104" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> The <em>William</em> is lading for England, and will sail about April 10 or 12. No intelligence yet to hand from Masulipatam. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Nathaniel Mountney, Nathaniel Wyche, and William Fremlen at Ahmadābād to the President and Council at Surat, March</span> 28, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 577).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Send an invoice of the goods speeded on the 25th. Yesterday forwarded to Agra bills for Rs. 12,000, and hope to remit a further sum of Rs. 8,000 shortly. Their broker has heard from that city that the English caravan is detained for the clearing of the saltpetre. This commodity can now be bought here for Rs. 1½ per maund. Tottle will bring samples of new Sarkhej indigo, the price of which is Rs. 12¾ and Rs. 14¼ [per maund]. Calicoes are also cheap. The goods for Agra depart out of the town to-morrow. ‘Mirza Muckey’ has deferred his departure, but, as there is a ‘caphila’ about to start, they will not wait for him. The looking-glass intended for Agra has been detained, as it needs repairs, and then they hope to sell it here. Send a copy of Mr. Hopkinson’s ledger. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received April</em> 2.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-330"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="330" data-folio="276" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n329_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 276">[p. 276]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Christopher Brown</span>, aboard the <em>William</em> at Swally, to the President and Council at Surat, March 29, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 576).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received their letters and will deliver them to ‘the comandore Johnson’ [<em>see</em> note on p. 182], whom he visits to-day. Thanks the President for a quilt. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, April 2, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 576).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The rough weather has hindered their lading. Thinks the ship had better go over the bar, in case the monsoon comes sooner than is expected. Would be glad of two native boats to help in getting off the goods. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, April 3, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 580).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The damaged sugar will be used in the ship. The weather has improved. Mr. Martin should be instructed to provide them with carts early in the morning, which is the best time for work. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Same to the Same</span>, April 4, 1628 (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 585).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Requests them to hasten the sending down of goods. (<em>Copy.</em> <sup>1</sup>/<sub>2</sub> <em>p. Received April 5.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Kerridge, Richard Wylde, and John Skibbow at Surat to the President and Council at Bantam</span>, April 7, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Java</em>, vol. iii. pt. ii. p. 455).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-105" href="#sec-08-fn-105" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-105" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">According to promise [<em>see</em> p. 225], they have again called Charles Clark to account for 375 rials said to be due to the estate of ‘Elyas Kudryan’; his answer is contained in the accompanying letter. <em>P.S.</em>—Have sent a few lines by the Dutch admiral, dated March 28, 1628; also 12 pieces of fine dyed calicoes. (<em>Copy. 1 p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Christopher Brown</span>, aboard the <em>William</em> [at Swally], to the President and Council at Surat, April 9, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. cii. p. 585).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The hold was shut before their letter arrived, but the pepper shall be stowed in the lower gunroom. All the saltpetre left out, besides some gum-lac, and a bale of indigo that got wet. Nine <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-331" data-page="331" data-folio="277" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n330_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 277">[p. 277]</button> jars of lamp-oil landed; the rest they must keep. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p. Received April</em> 10.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT TO THE COMPANY APRIL 14, 1628 (<em>Public Record Office: East Indies</em>, vol. iv. no. 27).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They know not what to do, seeing the great disconformity of shipping to the stock sent out. The charge consumes both principal and profit. Two hundred thousand pounds more than they have would be little enough to dispatch the ships already here and leave a competent stock for investment. (<em>Extract only.</em> ⅛ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">COMMISSION AND INSTRUCTIONS FROM PRESIDENT WYLDE, JOHN SKIBBOW, GEORGE PAGE, AND RICHARD BARBER TO THOMAS KERRIDGE AND CHRISTOPHER BROWN, PROCEEDING TO ENGLAND IN THE <em>WILLIAM</em>, APRIL 14, 1628 (<em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 121).</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The season being far spent, they are to use all possible expedition in their voyage. ‘According therfore to our masters respects and power yearly [<em>sic</em>] given to the place of Presedencie in Surratt, continued and confirmed by the immediate letters pattents of His Majestie of England, we, the now President and Councell, to whom the ordering and mannaging of whatsoever concerneth the Honourable Companies bussines or affaires within our limited precincts is referred, and warranted by vertue of our foresaid Kings Majesties commission,’ appoint Kerridge to be ‘cheife commaunder’, ‘to governe with such authority during the present voyage as by virtue of the aforesaid our Kings Majesties letters pattents, addressed to the President and Councell of Surratt [<em>see</em> p. 65], doth warrant’; and he is ‘to ly in the great cabbin with Captain Christofer Browne’. The accommodation to be afforded to Martin, Benthall, and Tottle is referred to his discretion. The ship is to go straight to Mauritius to meet Captain Hall’s fleet, and, after taking in provisions, to sail with him until a separation becomes necessary. Whether a call shall be made at the Cape is left to their discretion. To be on the watch against attacks from enemies. All matters of importance to be decided by a council composed of Kerridge, Brown, Martin, Benthall, William Peirce (master), and John Hunter, Kerridge having a casting vote.
