On October 24, finding that they had enough goods to fill the <em>Hart</em> also, it was decided to send her home; and accordingly they began to lade her. On the 7th instant, however, the Moors, having many goods left over after filling the ships intended for Persia, demanded that the promise of sending seven vessels thither should be fulfilled. Having forced the Dutch to land much rice, tobacco, and cotton wool to take in native goods instead<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-134" href="#sec-08-fn-134" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-134" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, they on the 8th sent two of their principal merchants, Hari Vaisya and Vīrjī Vōra, to whom the English were indebted over 30,000<em>l.</em>, with a message that while they would not (as they might) forcibly prevent the lading of the ships, yet unless their request was granted they would insist on their debts being paid before the goods were put on board for England. As there was no money available for this purpose, no course remained but to unload the <em>Hart</em> again and appoint her for Persia. After all this <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-361" data-page="361" data-folio="307" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n360_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 307">[p. 307]</button> is as well, for they have since heard that one caravan of 195 camels expected from Agra is detained on the way, owing to want of money to pay tolls; while of the second from the same place, which cannot be less than 700 camels, no news has come to hand. Probably they will not be here before the middle or end of February, and it would have been a pity to detain the ships till then. They will inquire into the cause of the delay, which is in contravention to their orders, and will recur to the subject in a later clause, ‘where wee shall diswade you from the continewance of that factory any longer.’ In response to a demand from Bantam, they dispeeded thither on November 12 the <em>Mary</em> and the <em>John</em>. Account of their lading. Should the Company determine to revive that trade, it would be advisable, instead of sending rials thither direct, to forward them, in the first instance, to Surat, where they might be invested in goods that would produce 100 per cent. profit or more at Bantam. A ship should also be dispeeded direct to Masulipatam (as is done by the Dutch) to purchase goods and then go on to Bantam; ‘for uppon theise two factories will and must your sotherne trade be grounded, if ever you meane to reape proffitt thereby.’ Another convenience would be that letters could be sent overland from Masulipatam to Surat, giving early intelligence of the Company’s plans. ‘This the Dutch had this yere, and had prevented us of the Amadavad indico, had wee not gathered by circomstances from theire owne discourse of their intents... Wee gave presente order to your servants in Amadavad to goe throughe for the whole parcell of old indico of the last yeres groweth, which wee weare ascertained to be extraordinary good and in opinnion of some of our people and brokers is little inferiour to that made in Agra.’ There is small likelihood of a fall in price, unless the Armenian and Moor merchants forbear their buying for Persia, &amp;c., where it is in much request, not less than 1,500 bales having been laden on the Dutch and English ships this year. The Governor will not allow them to sell lead to any one but himself; but this is avoided by sending it to Ahmadābād and trucking it for indigo. Proceed to answer the Company’s letter received by the <em>Jonah</em> and <em>Expedition</em>; but first intimate the loan to the Dutch of twenty-five barrels of gunpowder, and enclose Van den Broeck’s letter to his masters for payment of the amount in England. Note the receipt of the Surat letter of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-362" data-page="362" data-folio="308" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n361_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 308">[p. 308]</button> November 29, 1626, per Vincent Harris; also that the <em>London</em> and <em>Reformation</em> had arrived in safety. Perceive that the original of that letter was not delivered to the Company until January 14, 1628, wherein as in all things the Dutch have abused them. Did not send a letter by the <em>Palsgrave</em> and <em>William</em> to Bantam, as they did not think there was a chance of its being forwarded. Regret that the late President at Bantam has incurred the Company's displeasure by sending the <em>Abigail</em> to the Coast and in her Mr. Man, 'as you conceive to invest his great estate in diamonds.' Are persuaded that this information is wrong, for Mr. Man, who now goes passenger in the <em>Star</em>, protests that he did not buy a single diamond. Mr. Kerridge has no doubt explained the detention of the <em>Dolphin</em>. They very well apprehend that the life of trade consists in quick and speedy returns, and they wish the Company were as ready to find the means as they are to procure the goods. Had the commodities purchased in Agra and Ajmer come down in time for these ships, the value sent home this season would have exceeded that of the supply received by the <em>Jonah</em>, &amp;c. Trust that the dispatch of the <em>Mary</em> to Bantam will be approved. By her was forwarded the Company's letter addressed to that presidency. Regret that the excess of private trade to the southwards has led the Company to suspect them as well; protest that there is only a little here and there, and that it is of no great prejudice to the general trade, as without it some of the ships would have gone empty. They are doing their best to suppress private trade, as will be seen from the consultations; but it is a disease that must not be purged by violent and strong medicines, lest the whole body be endangered. The main hindrance thereunto must come from the Company at home by restraining their seamen, without whom the factors can do nothing. The latter think themselves as much entitled as the former to make use of their opportunities, especially as most of them are obliged, for the credit of their nation, to spend in apparel, &amp;c., more than their pay, and if they cannot make it up in some honest manner they must either go home poorer than they came or else wrong the Company by some indirect and dishonest courses. No doubt the Company has heard of the removal of their factors from Batavia to Bantam, owing to the 'insolency' of the Dutch. They trust that His Majesty will now at <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-363" data-page="363" data-folio="309" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n362_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 309">[p. 309]</button> last provide both for the satisfaction of past wrongs and the establishment of a firm and peaceable trade, which here will never be performed by the Dutch unless the King takes the Company under his protection. ‘Wee in theise parts live uppon faire and freindly termes with them; yet doe wee not lett to thinke it is more for want of power to doe us wronge then will to effect itt; nor (to say the truth) hath there beene want of will or power in us, had wee warrant for our accion.’ Acknowledge that the powder bought in Ahmadābād was poor, but the want thereof for the ships could not be neglected. It was bought by Hopkinson, who will doubtless satisfy the Company that he did his best. The information given by William Langford ‘is only a seamans advice, who will persuade you (as wee said before) to beleive anythinge, though never so falce; for wee are assured that in noe part of India is made so good powder as is made in England, neither by the Portugalls themselves, much lesse by the heathens and Indians in theise parts’.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-135" href="#sec-08-fn-135" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-135" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Their wants can be more quickly supplied from home than from the Coromandel Coast, for, first, it is uncertain whether the authorities there will permit the export of any quantity, and, secondly, the Masulipatam factors will not comply with requisitions from Surat without instructions from Bantam, whither also the powder must be sent, when ready, for transport to this place. As regards ‘the private trade of strangers and their passage uppon our shipps’, they have forbidden it except in the case of Persia. [<em>Four pages missing.</em>] A large quantity of gum-lac·sent home; will endeavour to procure some of ‘that sort in shells’ taken to England by Robert Young, which is only to be had in Lahore and thereabouts. No more aloes Socotrina will be supplied until further orders. Send twenty quilts, which had been put in hand before the Company’s letter arrived, but will abstain from further purchases. Note the orders for ‘the discontinewance of your Persian trade’; yet, as already explained, they have thought it well to send the ships thither with freight goods, which ought to yield the Company at least 10,000<em>l.</em> in customs. Have also laden some tobacco and cotton wool on a junk of the King’s, besides some rice, &amp;c., in the English ships. These goods <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-364" data-page="364" data-folio="310" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n363_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 310">[p. 310]</button> are under the charge of Richard Predys, and are not intended as a supply to the factors in Persia. If the jewels belonging to Mr. Bateman and Capt. Styles do not sell in Ahmadābād (where they now are), they shall be returned to England. Would have forwarded ‘Mr. John Milhoards salt’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-136" href="#sec-08-fn-136" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-136" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> to the same city, ‘but that place being empty of the nobillity that usually lay there and are now with the new Kinge,’ they thought it useless to do so, especially as they have no hope of selling it at anything like its invoice price. The Governor of Surat did not value it at above 500 rupees, ‘not esteeming at all the curiosities therein, which as they doe not understand the course of the plannetts and use of the dyall etc. so is the salt itselfe altogether unusefull with theise people.’ They will probably be obliged to return it on the <em>Hart</em>. The coral received is still in the customhouse, and they have been too busy to examine it. From the invoice they fear that it is of the same sort as that brought last year by Captain Hall, which lay unsold until this month, when Vīrjī Vōra bought the lot at 36 per cent. under the customs valuation (100 per cent. above cost). It was of poor quality compared with that brought by the <em>Palsgrave</em> and <em>William</em>. They hope to get rid of the present consignment in barter for pepper or indigo. The quicksilver arrived in good condition. Half has been sent to Ahmadābād in response to an offer of Rs. 95 per maund, though when it got thither the offer was reduced to Rs. 90; the rest has been sold here at Rs. 95 per maund, with four months’ credit. The goods now provided were purchased in Agra by Gregory Clement, etc.; in Ahmadābād by Nathaniel Mountney; in Broach by Richard Barber; in Baroda by Nathaniel West; and in Surat by Henry Glascock. A Clerk of the Stores having been thought necessary, Thomas Wilbraham has been appointed to that post. As regards the factors sent out in the last fleet, Richard Boothby has been taken into Council, and now follows the custom-house pending a better employment; John Willoughby has made two journeys to Ahmadābād to convoy gold and silver, an employment fittest for his humour; and Peter Mundy, for want of other work, has been employed in the office. Many time-expired servants <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-365" data-page="365" data-folio="311" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n364_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 311">[p. 311]</button> are returning, ‘here being small or noe imployment for them—those your new and yet in breath sitting still or doeing little.’ Joseph Hopkinson goes home after ten years’ service and will, they hope, be graciously welcome. They have paid him his third of this year’s wages. John Bangham is sent home for the better satisfaction of Sir Francis Crane; his accounts are not yet cleared. Robert Clitherow returns, being desirous to see his country and friends and there being little or nothing for him to do; he has been allowed 700 mahmūdīs ‘to cleare the country’. William Fall has, since his return from Mokha, been helping Thomas Joyce at the Marine; his abilities deserve better entertainment, and they have given him 100 mahmūdīs ‘towards sea provisions’. William Knightley has been employed in copying letters. His sufferings at Mokha deserve favour; they have given him 100 mahmūdīs to prepare for the voyage and have lent him a similar sum on account of wages. Jeremy Shuker and Malachi Martin have gone to Bantam on the <em>Mary</em>. The paper and quills sent this year are exceedingly bad. Request paper for their account books and ‘your customary favour in the provition of sack for the factoryes’, as the ships will not spare them any except with ‘grumbling and mutteringe’. Accounts of Eustace Man. The tapestry last sent lies unsold at Lahore. ‘There is of late quantety made and more comodious for tents then is that you send’; so no more should be furnished. The Governor of Surat sends patterns for sword blades ‘to be made in Germany’; if these can be supplied by the next ships they may excuse a greater present to him. Weight of pepper on the <em>Star</em> and <em>Exchange</em>; any loss must be due to theft or to its drying on the way. ‘The Portugall forces, as wee said before, are incerten, neither doe wee knowe where they lye; only wee are advertized that the Viceroy is aryved at Goa with two galloones,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-137" href="#sec-08-fn-137" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-137" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and other three are wantinge and not any newes of them. Other five galloones weare preparing in Goa, but for want of men put not to sea untill the now supply came; and all now are me fittinge for Muscatt with provisions of victualls and powder and shott, doubting the Persian intends to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-366" data-page="366" data-folio="312" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n365_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 312">[p. 312]</button> assault itt this yere; but wee beleeve they have other ends, well knowing the Shawe Abausse to be busied in his owne defence against the Turke. If therefore your forces be not stronge the next yere, your fleet will run great hazard uppon theire aryvall on this coast, where if they come seperated (as often they doe) the danger wilbe soe much the more; for wee heare of great boasts this Vice Kinge giveth of our and the Dutches utter ruine in theise parts. But wee hope it wilbe his tourne first; at least wee will indeavour it, if able and not prevented by your contradiction.’ Forward a packet from the factors at Armagon, giving intelligence of their removal from Masulipatam, for want of supply for its continuance. Fear they will not find it easy to gain re-admittance, as the Dutch will work their hindrance either by force or bribes. Think they would have done well to have complained to the King before departing and to have asked his permission to quit the country; he could not well have refused to right their wrongs, but if he did they would have had a better excuse for withdrawing. Their project of stopping the junks would then have been more defensible; in any case it should not be put in execution unless it is decided to abandon all intention of resettling. (<em>The rest is wanting. 12 pp. Mutilated.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">David Davis’s Account of the Voyage of the</span> <em>HART</em> <span class="sc">to Gombroon and back</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xliv. p. 87).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>December</em> 8. The <em>Hart</em> was appointed to go to Persia. <em>December</em> 9. Put ashore some of the lading she had taken in for England. <em>December</em> 11. ‘The <em>Litle James</em> was burnt by 17 Portugall frigotts, with the losse of 25 men and two barges, viz. the <em>Harts</em> barge with 5 men and the <em>Stars</em> barge with 5 men.’ <em>December</em> 27. The fleet, consisting of nine English ships, six Dutch, eleven Gujarātī junks and frigates, sailed from Swally. <em>December</em> 30. The <em>Exchange</em>, <em>Blessing</em>, and <em>Star</em> departed for England. 1629, <em>January</em> 23. Anchored off Jask, and sent ashore for news. <em>January</em> 27. Reached Gombroon, and the agent, Mr. Burt, came aboard. <em>February</em> 15. ‘The newes came that the King of Persia was dead.’<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-138" href="#sec-08-fn-138" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-138" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>February</em> 21. The English fleet sailed. <em>February</em> 23. They were joined by the Dutch and four Surat junks. <em>March</em> 8. <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-367" data-page="367" data-folio="313" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n366_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 313">[p. 313]</button> Saw the coast near Diu. <em>March</em> 12. Went into Swally Hole, and the President came on board the <em>Jonas</em>. (5 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account</span>, <span class="sc">by Peter Andrews</span> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. xlix. p. 45).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>December</em> 27. The fleet sailed. <em>December</em> 30. The homeward-bound ships parted company. 1629, <em>January</em> 15. Saw the Persian coast. <em>January</em> 23. The <em>Hopewell's</em> boat brought news from Jask that fourteen Portuguese galleons were looking for them. <em>January</em> 27. Reached Gombroon. <em>February</em> 21. Sailed again, but decided to wait for the Dutch, who came out the following day. <em>March</em> 8. Saw land near Diu. <em>March</em> 12. Anchored in Swally Hole. (6½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account, by Daniel Hall in the</span> <em>EXPEDITION</em> (<em>Ibid.</em>, vol. l. p. 15).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>December</em> 25. The <em>Expedition</em> went out of Swally Hole. <em>December</em> 26. The <em>Jonas</em> and the rest of the ships followed. <em>December</em> 27. Sailed. <em>December</em> 30. The <em>Exchange, Blessing</em>, and <em>Star</em> left for England. 1629, <em>January</em> 15. Land seen. <em>January</em> 23. Anchored off Jask, sailing again next morning. <em>January</em> 27. Anchored in Gombroon road. <em>February</em> 9. Went over to Ormus for ballast. <em>February</em> 14. Returned. <em>February</em> 21. Sailed. <em>March</em> 8. Land seen. <em>March</em> 12. Anchored in Swally Road. (5 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Abraham Sayers's Account of his Voyage to England in the</span> <em>STAR</em> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xlvi. p. 56).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>December</em> 26. Came out of Swally Hole. <em>December</em> 27. The fleet sailed. <em>December</em> 30. The <em>Exchange, Blessing</em>, and <em>Star</em> left the Persia fleet about 20 leagues off Diu Head. 1629, <em>March</em> 6. Saw the Coast near Cape Agulhas. [<em>Sketch.</em>] <em>March</em> 12. Anchored in Table Bay. <em>March</em> 20. Departed. <em>April</em> 5. Reached St. Helena. <em>April</em> 13. Sailed again. <em>July</em> 3. Off the Isle of Wight they saw eight sail coming out of Portsmouth. <em>July</em> 4. These ships proved to be Dunkirkers. Two of them came up, pretending to be from Leith, but they made no attempt <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-368" data-page="368" data-folio="314" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n367_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 314">[p. 314]</button> to attack. <em>July</em> 5. Anchored in the Downs. <em>July</em> 9. Sailed again. <em>July</em> 12. Anchored at Erith. (29½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Another Account by John Pashley</span> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. xlviii. p. 84).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1628, <em>December</em> 27. Sailed from Swally. The fleet consisted of six English ships (<em>Jonas</em>, <em>Hart</em>, <em>Christopher</em>, <em>Eagle</em>, <em>Hopewell</em>, and <em>Expedition</em>) bound for Persia, and three (<em>Star</em>, <em>Exchange</em>, and <em>Blessing</em>) for England: six Dutch ships for Persia: and some Surat junks. <em>December</em> 30. Parted with the Persian fleet. 1629, <em>February</em> 1. It was decided not to call at the Comoro Islands, for fear of losing the monsoon. <em>March</em> 6. Saw the African Coast. <em>March</em> 12. Anchored in 'the bay of Salldayny'. <em>March</em> 20. Sailed again. <em>April</em> 5. Anchored at St. Helena. <em>April</em> 13. Departed. <em>June</em> 8. Mounted their guns again. <em>July</em> 1. Saw Scilly. <em>July</em> 5. Anchored in the Downs. (18 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">The Names and Salaries of the Factors in the Indies</span> (<em>Public Record Office</em>: <em>East Indies</em>, vol. iv. no. 67).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-139" href="#sec-08-fn-139" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-139" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Surat: 'Richard Wilde, President, 100<em>l.</em>; John Skibbowe, 200<em>l.</em>; Richard Boothbye, 100<em>l.</em>; George Page, 100<em>l.</em>; Arthur Suffild, pursser, 50<em>l.</em>; John Willoughbie, 50<em>l.</em>; Nicholas Woolley, pursers mate, 30<em>l.</em>; Henrie Glascocke, 50<em>l.</em>; Raph Rande, a writer, 35<em>l.</em>; John Webb, a writer, 20<em>l.</em>; Peeter Mondaie, a writer, 30<em>l.</em>; Cutbert Charles, a writer [        ]; Crispen Blagden, a writer, 40<em>l.</em>; Thomas Smith, a writer, 25<em>l.</em>; Clement Dunscombe, a writer, 20<em>l.</em>; Thomas Joice, 33⅓<em>l.</em>; Robert Davison, steward, 20<em>l.</em>; John Calfe, a writer, 20<em>l.</em>; Thomas Wilborne,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-08-fn-140" href="#sec-08-fn-140" data-fn-target="sec-08-fn-140" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Mr. Wilds man, 20<em>l.</em>; George Turner, an unprofitable chirurgion, 40<em>l.</em>; John Blewe, cooke, 18<em>l.</em>; William Wade, a boye [        ]; two bakers, 36<em>l.</em>' Agra: 'Gregory Clement, 100<em>l.</em>; Raph Cartwrite, 40<em>l.</em>; William Fremling, 30<em>l.</em>; Thomas Aldworth, 30<em>l.</em>' Ahmadābād: 'Nathaniel Mounteny, 40<em>l.</em>; Nathaniel Weych, 66⅔<em>l.</em>; Henrie Graves, 15<em>l.</em>' <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-369" data-page="369" data-folio="315" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories03fost/page/n368_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 315">[p. 315]</button> Broach : ' Henrie Barbor, 100<em>l.</em> ; John Norris, 80<em>l.</em>' Baroda : ' Nathaniel West, 80<em>l.</em> ; William Price, 30<em>l.</em>' . . . Armagon : ' George Brewin, 150<em>l.</em> ; Thomas Johnson, 100<em>l.</em> ; Larance Henly, 40<em>l.</em> ; Nicholas Bixe, 80<em>l.</em> ; Edward Chapman, 60<em>l.</em> ; Arthur Fowkes, 40<em>l.</em> ; Leonard Couch, 12<em>l.</em> ; Egglesfild [     ].' (1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-08-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Van den Broeck mentions the appointment (April 1625), of ‘ Wollebrant Gelijnsz.’ to be chief at Broach (<em>Voyagie</em>, p. 105).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Adam Verhoeve, who signs a letter at Surat in May 1628 (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. viii. no. 278).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is an extract from this letter at the Public Record Office (<em>East Indies</em>, vol. iv. no. 27).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the 1618–21 volume, p. xxx, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dābhol was the principal port of the Bījāpur kingdom. The monarch here referred to was Ibrāhīm Ādil Shāh II, who died in 1626 (the same year as Malik Ambar), and <span class="folio-turn">[p. 193]</span> was succeeded by Mahmūd Ādil Shāh, then in his sixteenth year. I have not found any corroboration of the statement here made, that Dābhol was captured in that year by the Nizām Shāhī (Ahmadnagar) forces.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The following extract from the Court minutes of February 28, 1627, is apposite in this connexion: ‘Mr. Ellam desired to know the resolucion of the Court how the persons of the Portugalls should be dealt withall, if it should please God they should falle into the hands of the English. Some were of opinion to deale with them as Ruy Friero did with the English; some to put the cheife officers to death; but the gennerall resolucion was to shew themselves Englishmen and to be mercifull to all but Ruy Friero, and to shew the like crueltie to him as he did to the English; unlesse any new cruelties have since that bin pursued, and then the buisnes is left to the Commanders if they shall surprise any Portugalls in heate of bloud to doe what they thinke good, either by putting them to death, ransoming them (if persons of qualitie), or bringing them home prisoners; and for the meaner sort to keepe them in irons or make them to worke, or change one man for another if they have any of ours.’</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The meaning is that the authorities would insist on maintaining the present system, because under it no curiosity likely to give pleasure at court could escape their notice. For an instance of severe punishment following an omission to report the arrival of an acceptable picture see the 1618–21 volume, p. 111.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Balass rubies. The name is derived from <em>Badakhsī</em>, i. e. ‘of Badakhshān,’ the country whence they came.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is worth noting, as showing that the Company were doing all they could to pacify the clamour raised in England against their constant exportation of money.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A tombstone to his memory still exists in the Old Protestant Cemetery in the Civil Lines at Agra (<em>List of Christian Tombs and Monuments in the N. W. Provinces</em>, p. 173; <em>Transactions of the Archaeological Society of Agra</em>, 1875, p. xv). The inscription, which is much worn, appears to have run: ‘[Here lies the body] of Justinian Off[ey, wh]o was chief marchant in Agra fo[r the Engli]sh. He deceased [the 18th of] Aprill, 1627.’ Close at hand are two other English tombs (John Drake, 1637, and George Purchas, 1651) and four Dutch.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kalhāt, about twenty miles N. W. of Sūr; burnt by Albuquerque in 1507.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Evidently the factors at Armagon had commenced to coin pagodas.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Dagh-Register</em> (p. 326) gives the value of their cargo as 930,115 florins.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Of Olpād.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khwāja Abūl Hasan.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. the waterside. ‘When any ships ride at Swalley (from September to March commonly), the bannians all along the sea side pitch booths or tents and straw houses in great numbers, where they sell callicoes, Chena satten, purcellan ware, scrutores or cabbinets of mother of pearle, of ebony, of ivory, agats, turquoises, heliotropes, cornelians, rice, sugar, plantans, arack, &amp;c.’ (Herbert’s <em>Travels</em>, ed. 1638, p. 37).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Van den Broeck (<em>Voyagie</em>, p. 107) says that he heard of the event on December 6 (N. S.=November 26, O. S.).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to the native historians, Jahāngīr died near Rājor about breakfast-time on Sunday, 28 Safar, 1037 A. H. (Elliot’s <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 435, vol. vii. p. 5; Blochmann, <em>Calcutta Review</em>, vol. xlix. p. 127; and information kindly furnished by Mr. Henry Beveridge). This is usually stated to be equivalent to October 28, 1627 (O. S.), which certainly was a Sunday; but the ordinary tables make A. H. 1037 commence on September 2, 1627, and in that case 28 Safar would fall on October 29. However, Indian chronology has its obscurities, and in the present case the day of the week appears to be our safest guide.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khānazād Khān (Mīrzā Amān-ullah). Shāh Jahān, soon after his accession, gave him the title of Khān Zamān Bahādur. He died in 1637 (Beale). De Laet (p. 274) confirms the account given in the text of Mahābat Khān's movements. He says that it was Āsaf Khān who gave secret orders to the royal troops not to make an attack.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This conflicts with the account given by Van den Broeck (<em>Voyagie</em>, p. 107), for he declares that he was the first to visit the Prince from Surat. He was at Swally when he heard that Shāh Jahān was approaching and that a demand had been made on the Dutch merchants for Rs. 10,000 for his use. He at once returned to Surat, and the same evening went with a present to the Prince's camp, which was pitched about three (Dutch) miles outside the city. He was very graciously received, and was given a fine horse (to which Mahābat Khān added a second). At Van den Broeck's request Shāh Jahān gave the Dutch a fresh farmān. Herbert (ed. 1638, p. 108) says, speaking of the Prince, 'at our being in his country he came within two dayes journey of Surrat, and in a ceremoniall way the English merchants ships thundred out his health by 200 great shot, which he most thankfully accepted of.'</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Muhammad, son of Khwāja Shāhsuwār, a merchant who accompanied Naqd Alī Beg to England. As already mentioned (p. 88 <em>n.</em>), the father died during the mission. The son embarked on the return voyage but, as stated in the text, died before reaching India (see Herbert, p. 32).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Maagd van Dort</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘The Portugalles ships were destroyed by fowle weather’ (<em>marginal note</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These were probably wanted for presentation to the King; see a note at p. 199 of the 1618–21 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A marginal note explains that this plate had been shipped by mistake to Surat instead of Batavia, being intended for the new factory at Lagundy.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See pp. 183, 185; also Herbert’s <em>Travels</em> (ed. 1638), p. 11.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Aboard the <em>Palsgrave</em> . . . ready to sett sayle from Sualy Roade.’</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Enclosed in the Surat letter of January 4 (see p. 213). The criticisms had reference to the accounts sent home by the <em>Great James</em> in 1626.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Vianen</em>, belonging to the fleet which left Batavia in November, 1627, under the command of the late Governor-General, Pieter de Carpentier. She had lost company with the rest and, having been blown out of her course, had coasted about two hundred miles along the northern side of Australia (see Major's <em>Early Voyages to Terra Australis</em>, pp. lxxxix, 45).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For whom see the 1618–21 volume, pp. 223, 291, where he is called Fiorino.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Also called 'Elias Kudryan' (see p. 276, and the Court Minutes of August 27, 1628).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For these two pamphlets see the <em>Calendar of State Papers, East Indies, 1625–29</em>, pp. 306, 308, &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nobles (Arabic <em>umarā</em>, pl. of <em>amīr</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently this building has not been identified. De Laet (p. 277) says that Shāh Jahān 'in proprio palatio, quod loco amoenissimo ad ripas Gemenae flu. condiderat, tantisper subsedit, donec auspicato die in arcem ingrederetur, et thronum regium occuparet'.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīrzā Khān, Khānkhānān, had died a short time before; and the title was now transferred to Mahābat Khān, in recognition of his services to the new emperor.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Tāptī. Variao is on the northern bank, about three miles from Surat on the road to Broach.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>sarāī</em> or lodging-house for travellers.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Bhilāpur, 10 miles south-east of Baroda.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was all false.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Kifāyat Khān (see the 1622–23 volume, p. 218).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Shāh Salīm, i. e. Jahāngīr. The reference to the premature coining of money at Surat in the name of Shāh Jahān is interesting.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>ablaq</em>, a piebald (horse).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Portuguese <em>cavalleiro</em>, a rider.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">This seems to be a mistake for Khānazād Khān (see note on p. 204). The writer has confused him with his father, the Khānkhānān (p. 229).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">Salutation (Arabic <em>salām</em>, 'peace').</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. Beveridge thinks that this is the Arabic <em>dawā</em>, 'a call or invocation,' meaning that in the <em>khutba</em> (see p. 240) the name of Bulāqī (Dāwar Bakhsh) was used as that of the reigning sovereign.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Gold mohurs; cf. ‘vast sumes of gold in mores or sunnies’ (<em>Hedges’ Diary</em>, vol. ii. p. 326). Apparently the word is derived from the Hind. <em>sonā</em>, ‘gold.’</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. (from Arabic) <em>naubat</em>, the palace music.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Perhaps the <em>Mūsā</em> (‘Moses’) and <em>Safī</em> (‘Peace’).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Sic.</em> The <em>sikkā</em> rupee, for which see the 1618–21 volume, p. 113, may be meant.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Port. <em>jagra</em> (ultimately from Sanskrit <em>sarkarā</em>), a coarse sugar made from the sap of the palm-tree.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. passing them free through the customhouse under the pretence that they belonged to an Englishman.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">William Tozer, master of the <em>Palsgrave</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the story in Herbert’s <em>Travels</em> (ed. 1638, p. 173). According to him, the Prince’s name was ‘Codobanda [Khudā-banda] Sultan’ and that of his daughter, whom he slew in a frenzy, ‘Fatyma.’ Herbert says that the Prince had been blinded by drawing ‘a flaming steele afore his eyes’.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A piece of a bamboo cane cut off below a joint and then sealed at the top. Until the general introduction of glass bottles, these easily made receptacles were commonly used for the conveyance even of liquids.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently what is meant is a substantial warehouse with a terrace roof.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>khutba</em> is the address delivered after divine service, during which the preacher blesses Muhammad, his successors, and the reigning sovereign. The introduction of a fresh name was equivalent to the proclamation of a new monarch.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Elliot’s <em>History</em>, vol. vi. p. 438. The victims were Shahriyār, Dāwar Bakhsh (Bulāqī), and his brother Garshāsp, with Tahmūras and Hoshang, sons of the deceased Prince Dāniyāl. De Laet (p. 277) says that the actual murderer was ‘Radia Bahador’, and Herbert identifies him with the murderer of Khusrū; but on this see pp. 241–2.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mīrzā Barkhurdār Khān Ālam was made Governor of Bihār soon after Shāh Jahān’s accession (Blochmann’s <em>Āīn</em>, vol. i. p. 513).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hājīpur-Patna. It is on the opposite side of the river to Patna.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The rumour was of course untrue so far as Nūr Mahal (Nūr Jahān) was concerned.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Household: <em>khānazād</em>, ‘born in the house.’</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Nothing seems to be known concerning the son (<em>cf.</em> p. 240) or sons of Parwīz here referred to.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">This would mean the Rānā Karan of Udaipur. Mr. Beveridge says that according to the <em>Maāsir-ul-Umarā</em> Karan died in A. H. 1038 (Aug. 1628–Aug. 1629); and if this date be correct, the rumour given above must have been unfounded, like the one about the death of Shāh Abbās which immediately follows.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note 1 on p. 252.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Ultimo Febr.'; but it is possible that the 28th is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The candy was twenty maunds.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dārā Shikoh, Shujā, and Aurangzīb.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mahr. <em>tasū</em>, the twenty-fourth part of a 'covedo' (see p. 87).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a stone in the South African Museum, Cape Town, which bears an inscription recording the arrival of the <em>William</em> on September 1, and her departure on the 18th (<em>sic</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Cape Merchant of the saied fleete.’</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">It is clear, from the description given later, that the fortified island of Janjīrā, in the Rājpurī creek, is meant. Danda-Rājpurī is really the neighbouring town on the mainland.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sīdī Ambar (known as Sānak or ‘The Little’, to distinguish him from Malik Ambar) was Governor of Janjīrā from 1621 till 1636 (<em>Bombay roll, Gazetteer</em>, vol. xi. p. 436).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Dakhinī, i. e. Ahmadnagar. Della Valle in 1623 speaks of Danda-Rājpurī as belonging to Nizām Shāh, whom he elsewhere describes as ‘King of Dacan’.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A town a little way up the Shāstrī River. It is called ‘Sytuore’ and ‘Saitavda’ in the <em>Indian Atlas</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Khārepātan is about 25 miles up the Viziadrūg River, but the name was also applied (as here) to the town at the mouth (Viziadrūg or Gheria, later the famous stronghold of the Angrias), which served as its port.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the Devgad (Deoghar) River.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Della Valle mentions his passing by 'Tambona', to the south of 'the point of Carapetan' (Khārepātan, i. e. Viziadrūg). The <em>Indian Atlas</em> shows a village named Tembhavli, about four miles up the Devgad River. This river Dom João de Castro (<em>Roteiro</em>, p. 23) calls 'Rio de Tamaraa'. The fleet probably anchored at the mouth.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Ādil Shāh, the title of the king of Bījāpur (see p. 192).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Davis says 16° 30′, and Sayers 16° 24′. The latter is approximately correct.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">See p. 243. On p. 268 he is called a 'Portingall'.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">7.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Sibo Sibo' is a hopeless puzzle. By 'Avardar' is meant the Hind. <em>havildār</em>, familiar nowadays as a name for a sepoy non-commissioned officer. Among the Mahrattas, however, a commandant of a fort was so styled (<em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd ed. p. 412), and here the term is used in the general sense of 'a Governor'. Fryer applies <span class="folio-turn">[p. 255]</span> it to the Governor of 'Gullean' (Kalyan), and in his glossary explains that 'havaldar' means 'a commander'.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Mahmūd Hāmid.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Jaitāpur, the port of Rājāpur. It is on the Rājāpur River, four miles from its mouth. De Castro (<em>Roteiro</em>, p. 31) calls it 'Ceitapor', which is very near the form given in the text.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Andrews calls this place 'Gouncore' or 'Gauncore', and says it is eight or ten miles north of Jaitāpur. Evidently Purangad Creek, at the mouth of the Muchkundi River, is meant. There is a village named Goankhadi or Goankhuree near the river mouth.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Copies of the documents exchanged during these negotiations are given at p. 13 of the MS. The English demands included permission to build a castle in Khārepātan 'or anywhere else that we shall like better of': the gift of suitable houses at Rājāpur and other places where they might wish to establish factories: the fixing of definite prices for pepper and other commodities, and the guarantee of a certain yearly supply: an act of oblivion for the attacks on Dābhol: and leave to build two or three frigates yearly and to hire native rowers for the same. On his side the Governor wished to stipulate that the English should not capture any vessels bound to or from the ports of the Ādil Shāh, and should assist the latter in his wars with any nation in whose territories they had not settled factories; also that their ships should not enter the Rājāpur river, but ride outside. This last article was rejected as inadmissible; as regards the others, the English expressed their willingness to observe neutrality while in the King's ports, but insisted on their right to capture the vessels of the Portuguese and other enemies on the high seas without restriction; they were willing to assist the King in his wars, but on such terms as would secure them from loss.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The two forts on the mainland were named Aguada and Cabo respectively. The fortified island must be Secretario, but Predys is wrong in classing it as one of the Quiemados ('Burnt') Islands, which are considerably to the north.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">I. e. the fleet of the creek, Port. <em>calheta</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Anjidīv or Angediva, an island off Karwar, belonging to Portugal. It is chiefly remembered as the spot where the English troops, under the command of Sir Abraham Shipman, awaited the surrender of Bombay by the Portuguese. A description of the island was given by Mr. F. J. Varley, I. C. S., in the <em>Geographical Journal</em> for April, 1904.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Vilinjam, near Trivandrum; see a note on p. 1 of the 1618–21 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Predys gives a list of the ports on the coast from Swally to Cape Comorin, with their distances from one another.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Réunion. It was originally known as Mascarenhas, from the name of its Portuguese discoverer. In 1613 it was visited by the English interloper <em>Pearl</em>, under Captain Castleton, who gave it the name of England’s Forest, though it was sometimes called Pearl Island, after the ship (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 331). For a description see Herbert’s <em>Travels</em> (1638 edn., p. 351).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Oppagods' appears on Baffin's map (1619), a little to the south of 'Carapam' [Khārepātan]. This is copied from the map in the English edition of Linschoten, where it appears as 'Opagod'.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">[‘Bantan’ in margin.]</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In the <em>Mary</em> till July 10; after that in the <em>Hart</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">i. e. they put most of their guns into the hold, as there was little likelihood of fighting in those waters.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>banjārā</em>, itinerant dealers, mostly in grain and salt. They are often referred to under the name of <em>brinjarries</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See note on p. 240, <em>supra</em>, and the introduction to the previous volume, p. xxv.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Here (and elsewhere in the present volume) 'Rs.' means 'rupees', not 'rials'.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Antri, 12 miles south of Gwalior.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The duty levied was 1¼ mahmūdīs 4 pice per 100 mahmūdīs of estimated value; but on the goods for Ahmadābād the Customer was paid 360 mahmūdīs 'for undervalluing', while his subordinates received 37 mahmūdīs in fees; and similar bribes were given in the case of the caravan for Surat. There were further charges for porterage, carriage, duties at Ankleswar (56 mahmūdīs for 20 carts), &amp;c.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">'Four munsells [<em>manzil</em>, a stage] a this side Guallere' [Gwalior]. Narwār, 23 miles NE. of Sipri, is probably intended.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a second copy at p. 459 of the same volume. This letter was sent in one of the Dutch ships (see next page).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Bommel</em> and the <em>Wesp</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is another copy in the same volume (p. 464).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the account in <em>Marine Records</em>, vol. li.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Signed by Captain Matthew Morton, Joseph Hopkinson, John Norris, William Fall, John Roberts, John White, Lawrence Fisher, Francis Stockton, and Anthony Rumsy (?)</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Morton died in November, 1631. This narrative was no doubt found among his papers sent home.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a transcript made in 1787 see <em>Factory Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. ix. p. 169.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Purchas His Pilgrimes</em>, vol. i. pp. 320 <em>et seq.</em></span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Later on his name is given as ‘Mirpsse’ (<em>sic</em>). Mr. S. H. Bilgrami suggests Mīr Abbās.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Some words have evidently been omitted. Presumably the reference is to special customs charges (<em>see</em> later).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Kotwāl</em>, an official who was responsible for keeping order in the town.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘It should seeme then that the Dutch had free libertie to sell to whome they would and to buy of whome they pleased in Musilpa[tam], whereas we were in both restreined to the Governor only or his deputies.’ (<em>Marginal note, made in London.</em>)</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The last three letters are not quite distinct. 'Catamaran' (see the previous volume, p. 143) is intended.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was one of the master's mates.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Thomas Smith appears as 'Register' of these and the following consultations down to the end of the year.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hall, Morton, Swanley, Slade, Evans, Pashley, and Watts.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">John Hall, Matthew Morton, James Slade, Richard Swanley, Andrew Evans, John Pashley, Thomas Watts, Alexander Ball, William Minors, and Peter Cowian.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-120" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-120" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">July, according to the letter of November 17, which is no doubt correct.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-121" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-121" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Morris</em> arrived in England towards the end of October, 1628, and Hawley had then died.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-122" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-122" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Van den Broeck’s <em>Voyagie</em>, p. 108, and <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. viii. no. 279, and vol. ix. no. 298. The culprit’s name was Jan Abrahams.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-123" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-123" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Job Harby, or Harvey, one of the ‘Committees’ of the Company and a prominent London merchant. He became one of the Farmers of the Customs, and was knighted in December, 1637.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-124" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-124" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The royal proclamation of February 15, 1628, prohibiting private trade except so far as it may be licensed by the Company (see <em>Calendar of State Papers, E. Indies, 1625–9</em>, p. 466).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-125" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-125" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A second copy will be found at p. 511 of the same volume, and a third forms <em>O. C.</em> 1284.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-126" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-126" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Swanley, Slade, Evans, Pashley, Watts, and Cowlan.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-127" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-127" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">In other words, to be the vice-admiral of the squadron.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-128" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-128" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a second copy in <em>Factory Records, Surat</em>, vol. i. p. 137.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-129" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-129" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Omitted in the other copy (see postscript).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-130" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-130" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Omitted in the other copy.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-131" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-131" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Swanley, Slade, Pashley, Evans, Watts, Ball, Minors, and Cowlan.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-132" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-132" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is the last entry in this particular section, which was doubtless sent home in the <em>Exchange</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-133" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-133" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The date is deduced from a subsequent letter (<em>O.C.</em> 1292).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-134" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-134" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. ix. no. 298.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-135" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-135" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Langford had told the Company that gunpowder might be readily procured at Masulipatam for 3½<em>d.</em> per lb. and that it was unnecessary therefore to send out such large supplies (<em>Minutes</em>, Feb. 15, 1628).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-136" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-136" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">An elaborately ornamented salt-cellar which Capt. John Milward (one of the Committees) had been permitted in the preceding March to send to India for sale on his own account.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-137" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-137" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was wrong. No Viceroy arrived during 1628. The Viceroy Francisco da Gama returned to Lisbon this year by order from the King, leaving the government in the hands of the Bishop of Cochin, as Francisco Mascarenhas, who had been nominated to succeed to the post, had already left for Europe.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-138" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-138" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">According to Beale, Shāh Abbās died on January 8, 1629.</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-139" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-139" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This appears to be the list presented at a Court meeting held on February 13, 1629. The factors outside India are here omitted, but will be found in Mr. Sainsbury's Calendar (1625–9, p. 625).</span></li><li id="sec-08-fn-140" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-08-fn-140" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Elsewhere he is called Wilbraham.</span></li></ol></section>
